  - **Why now.** That is the owner brief's one pointless outcome (a loop that lets through what today's gate catches), and priority 5 puts deterministic over fast. The fix is one comparison with a function that already exists, inside the function D56 just changed.
  - **Why parking with no park between is not a false charge.** That gap is the runner's own time: the entry, the settings, and the code-index check, which can be seconds and not only milliseconds. A person who edits a control while an iteration runs is parked today, so a person who lands in the gap is treated the same.
  - **Why naming is enough across a park.** A park is the time the page gives a person to edit a control. Parking on it again would loop them, and no confirmation the runner could ask for is one a process cannot forge. The honest control there is that nothing changes silently.
  - **Standing rules.** Constitution I holds: nothing at the merge root or in CI changes and a check is added, none removed, so the owner brief's *Always ask a person* is not reached. Constitution III: no new state on disk, no setting, no dependency. Constitution VIII: the field is additive and optional, as D58's `bookkeeping` was.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** a real run is shown parking on a between-iterations difference that neither an iteration nor anything it started made — an editor, a hook or the code-index sync rewriting a control in the gap. Then that gap is named, not parked on, and the finding returns to a person as a question about containment. (High on the grade, the placement and parking with no park between; medium on naming only across a park, which is the weakest part and is stated as the residual.)
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/adversary-log.md` (S02's row, A2: HIGH, fixed in Phase 4, with the commit); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (S02's criteria, numbered by the host from AC-S02-70; D56's amended clause beside AC-S02-6); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/slices/S02-runner-bookkeeping/tasks.md` (the Phase 4 tasks); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (Parking Lot); `changelog.d/runner-bookkeeping.md`
- **Status:** standing


## D65 — May the gate newly refuse an entry that says `Status:` twice, or a log that opens with a byte-order mark, when a log written before this release can contain either?
- **Stage:** adversary (reading the fixes T026 and T027 returned) · **Slice:** S02-runner-bookkeeping · **When:** 2026-10-03T20:45:06Z · **Iteration:** 9
- **Question:** D63 said *an entry that says its scope or its status twice is refused by the gate*, and T026 and T027 built that, with a byte-order mark before the first entry made a heading the gate refuses. A `Scope:` line is new with this release, so refusing a second one falls on nothing a project already has. A second `- **Status:**` line, and a byte-order mark, can sit in a log an earlier factory's checker passes today; after `slipwai migrate` that project's `make verify` would turn red in CI for content it already had — the kind of new refusal D54 says is a person's to approve, and that D60 kept out of this slice (*no log written by any released factory changes its answer*; AC-S02-57).
- **Options:** (a) the gate refuses only what this release introduces — a second `Scope:` line, a malformed `Scope:` value; a second `Status:` line is a `note:` and exit 0, and the verb carries the entry whatever either line says; a byte-order mark at the start of the file is read past, by the gate and the verb, so the first entry is read as an entry — the standing decisions' answer (*settled when T028 met it:* reading past the mark in the gate changes its answer on a log with the mark directly before `## D1` and no title — the earlier checker never saw that entry — so **the gate reads such a log exactly as the earlier checker did, and only the verb reads past the mark**, printing and counting the first entry as an entry); (b) keep the refusals and ask a person — S02 would wait on an approval for two `LOW` findings; (c) keep them silently.
- **Decision:** (a). D63 is amended in that one clause and stands otherwise; AC-S02-83 and AC-S02-85 are reworded. The gate's verdict on a log that carries no `Scope:` line is held to the earlier checker's by a differential example over a set of such logs — a second `Status:` line, a byte-order mark, a separator character inside a field among them — with one stated exception: a log that is not UTF-8 ended on a traceback and exit 1 and now ends on one line and exit 1.
- **Why:** The developer who runs `migrate` must not find the gate red over a log it passed yesterday; what protects the skipper is the verb carrying what it cannot place, and that needs no refusal.
- **Decided by:** host (standing decision D60)
- **Confidence:** high · **Would reverse if:** a person approves the stricter gate, as they may for an entry with no `Scope:` line at all (the Parking Lot's question).
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (AC-S02-83, AC-S02-85), `specs/001-faster-slipwai/slices/S02-runner-bookkeeping/tasks.md` (T028)
- **Status:** standing


## D66 — Where does User Story 9 (a learned classifier routes workers per spec node) go in the split, and what becomes of the brief's line against switching routing on?
- **Stage:** split (a person's scope through `/cruise-tell`, read at the boundary after S02's done marker) · **Slice:** none (feature-level) · **When:** 2026-10-03T21:44:21Z · **Iteration:** 9
- **Question:** The owner committed User Story 9 to the specification at `cc91422` (FR-034 to FR-038, SC-012) and sent, in their words: *Owner scope addition: spec.md gained User Story 9 (FR-034 to FR-038): model routing is decided per spec node by a classifier, a JEV-style joint-embedding predictive model or an open-source local equivalent, calibrated on this repo's result contracts, with the rule-based policy as fallback; workers only, fixed tiers for planner/judge/skipper/adversary. Append it to the split after S18-route-log (depends_on S15, S16, S18). The owner brief's out-of-scope line about not switching routing on no longer applies to this story.* How is it cut, where does it sit, and how does the run read the brief from here?
- **Options:** (a) one slice; (b) two — the rule as a routing policy the owner can turn on, then the classifier with the rule as its fallback — after `S18-route-log`, as sent; (c) wait for the brief itself to be edited before splitting.
- **Decision:** (b), placed as sent. `S29-route-by-rule` (FR-034's `rule`, FR-037) and `S30-route-classifier` (FR-034's `model`, FR-035, FR-036, FR-038, SC-012) follow `S18-route-log`, each depending on `S15`, `S16` and `S18` as the owner said. For this story the owner brief's *Out of scope* line *Switching model routing on* and priority 6's *not switched on* are read as lifted, on the owner's word recorded here; the brief is the owner's file and the run does not edit it, so until they change it a skipper reads this entry beside it. What does not change: the run never changes a setting, so `route_by_difficulty` stays where the owner leaves it; planner, judge, skipper and adversary keep fixed tiers; nothing at the merge root or in CI moves. **The host's reading, not the owner's words:** the cut into two; `S30` also depending on `S29`, on `S14-result-contract` (FR-036 calibrates on result contracts) and on `S13-one-hop-brief` (FR-035's one-hop contracts); and that the classifier, being a new dependency whose reversal is a migration, gets an ADR at `Proposed` at its plan stage, never accepted by the run.
- **Why:** The owner's message and the specification they committed; two slices because the fallback is usable, and demonstrable, before any classifier exists (owner brief, priority 4).
- **Decided by:** human
- **Confidence:** high · **Would reverse if:** the owner wants one slice, another place, or the brief's line kept for the rule-only slice.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (the two rows, the Slice graph, S18's *Defers*, the Parking Lot)

## D67 — How many diagrams does one changed slice redraw, when the example says two and the global diagram contains every slice?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S11-render-once · **When:** 2026-10-03T21:47:26Z · **Iteration:** 10
- **Question:** The split's example for S11 says *one browser launches, two diagrams are rewritten*, and User Story 4's first scenario says *only that slice's and its segment's diagrams are rewritten*. `renderGlobalMermaid` in `assets/toolkit/scripts/event-model/mermaid.ts` draws every slice into the global diagram, so an edit to one slice changes three sources — the global one, its segment's and its own — and the split's second example already counts three for a one-slice model. Which stands: the count, or FR-012's rule?
- **Options:** (a) FR-012's rule stands and the count follows from it: exactly the diagrams whose Mermaid source changed are redrawn, which is three on the fixture — the stage's recommendation, since the specification's own requirement says it outright; (b) keep *two* by exempting the global diagram from the skip or from the redraw; (c) ask a person.
- **Decision:** (a). FR-012 is the requirement and *two* was arithmetic done without the global diagram. On the fixture's edit — one slice's own content, the frame count kept, nothing another slice reads — three diagrams are redrawn and 22 are left. An edit that adds or removes a frame, or that another slice reads, redraws every diagram whose source it changes, and no other; the criterion is the rule, and the fixture's three is its example. User Story 4's first scenario, its Independent Test and S11's row are reworded to say so; SC-004 stands as written.
- **Why:** A developer who changed one slice must see it in the whole timeline too; leaving the global picture as it was would be the stale diagram the stamp exists to prevent, and the owner brief's priority 5 calls a cache that could report a false current wrong.
- **Decided by:** host (stage recommendation)
- **Confidence:** high · **Would reverse if:** the owner meant the global diagram to be drawn some other way than on every change to a slice.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (User Story 4, S11's criteria); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (S11's row)
- **Status:** standing

## D68 — What may a skipped diagram rest on, so that `make model` never reports a stale or broken picture as current (FR-012)?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S11-render-once · **When:** 2026-10-03T21:49:31Z · **Iteration:** 10
- **Question:** FR-012 says `render.ts` re-renders only changed diagrams. What may a skip rest on: (1) the key, where a renderer record lives, and what happens to an SVG from an earlier factory; (2) a write cut short; (3) the PNG; (4) forcing a full redraw; (5) orphans, once the two directories are no longer deleted wholesale; (6) the text artifacts. Also: what a generated repository is asked to do after `slipwai migrate`, and whether the slice stays a PATCH.
- **Options:** (a) source hash alone, as today's stamp; (b) source hash plus a renderer key recorded beside the stamp in the SVG, an SVG without it redrawn once, atomic write, PNG always drawn when asked, no new setting, orphans removed by name, text artifacts written only when different — **the stage's recommendation**; (c) a sidecar manifest of keys.
- **Decision:** (b), the stage's recommendation, with five additions and one correction to the criterion's count. Each numbered rule is one a test can hold.
  1. **The skip rule.** A diagram is skipped only when all four hold; otherwise it is drawn.
     - Its SVG's first line is `<!-- em-source-sha256: <hash> -->`, carrying the hash of the Mermaid the model produces now. The `.mmd` on disk is never the evidence.
     - Its second line is `<!-- em-renderer-sha256: <key> -->`, carrying this run's renderer key.
     - The file ends with `</svg>` once trailing whitespace is trimmed.
     - No CI marker is set (`CI`, `GITHUB_ACTIONS` or `GITLAB_CI` non-empty, D32's list).
  2. **The renderer key.** It is one SHA-256 over:
     - the installed mermaid-cli version and the installed mermaid version, each read from its `package.json` under `scripts/event-model/.mermaid-cli/` (what is installed, not the `MERMAID_CLI` constant);
     - the bytes of `render.ts`, of `patch-mermaid-swimlanes.ts`, and of any other script the plan routes drawing through — the plan names the closed set, and it holds at least those two;
     - the bytes of the file `MERMAID_PUPPETEER_CONFIG` names, or a fixed word when it is unset.

     The width and the command line live in `render.ts`, so they are covered. `mermaid.ts` is not in the key, because a change there that matters changes the source hash.
  3. **Where the key is recorded.** In the SVG, as the second line. `extractHash` matches only `em-source-sha256: ` followed by 64 hex characters, so a line with another marker name leaves that reading unchanged. `page.ts` already inlines the first comment and will carry the second the same way. (c) is rejected: a manifest can vouch for a file that was replaced under it.
  4. **An SVG with no renderer line** (drawn by an earlier factory) is not current and is redrawn; that happens once.
  5. **A write cut short.** An SVG reaches its final name only by a rename of a complete file that already carries both lines. The temporary file sits in a directory the project's ignore list already covers, on the same filesystem; no ignore line is added. A draw that fails leaves the earlier file as it was and the run exits non-zero. The `</svg>` test in rule 1 covers a file torn by anything else.
  6. **The PNG.** Drawn whenever `PNG=1` or `--png` is given, never skipped, and written by the same rename. Not asked for, an existing `model.png` is left as today.
  7. **Forcing a redraw.** No setting and no flag. Deleting a diagram, or `segments/` and `slices/`, is the documented way: one sentence in `assets/toolkit/docs/event-model/README.md`.
  8. **Orphans.** Before anything is drawn, every entry in `segments/` and `slices/` whose name the current model does not produce is removed, file or directory, leftover temporaries included. The produced names are `model-<i>.mmd` and `model-<i>.svg` per segment, and `<slice id>.mmd` and `<slice id>.svg` per slice. The empty-model branch is unchanged.
  9. **Text artifacts.** Each `.mmd`, `model.html` and the README block is computed every run and written only when its bytes differ from the file on disk. A file left alone is not reported as written. `model.html` is built from the SVGs on disk after drawing.
  10. **The report.** The closing line says how many diagrams were drawn and how many were left, in place of *N slices rendered*.
  - **The count in the criterion is wrong and is the host's to reword.** The global diagram contains every slice, so one changed slice always changes at least three sources: the global, its segment and its slice. The spec's scenario 1 (*only that slice's and its segment's*) and the split's *two diagrams are rewritten* cannot hold. The rule is: exactly the diagrams whose Mermaid source hash changed are redrawn, and no other. Frame numbers are global, so a change that adds or removes a frame also redraws every later slice and segment. The fixture's edit must therefore keep the frame count, and its expected count is three.
  - **Unavailable:** whether three diagrams in one browser, plus the target's `npm install` step, finish under SC-004's 2 seconds. That is the slice's own measurement, not a decision.
  - **After `slipwai migrate`:** a generated repository is asked to do nothing. Its first `make model` redraws every diagram once, and since all of this output is ignored, nothing committed changes. A repository that removed those ignore lines and commits the diagrams sees one diff, the second comment line in each SVG, and commits it. The fragment says both.
  - **Level:** PATCH, as the split names. There is no new file, setting, flag or ignore line; `VERSION` stays `1.6.0.dev0`.
  - **Two things seen, for the Parking Lot:**
    - `mermaidCli()` installs only when the `mmdc` binary is absent, so a raised pin does not reinstall. Reading installed versions keeps the skip honest, but the renderer still lags the pin.
    - No Makefile target runs `check.ts`; `make check-model` runs `check.py`, which reads no stamp.
- **Why:** The developer reads these pictures to decide what a slice touches, and a picture that looks current and is not is worse than a slow one. The owner brief's priority 5 settles the key in its own words: a cache that could report a false current is wrong, its key includes tool versions and script hashes, and it is never trusted in CI. CI has nothing on disk to skip, so that rule costs nothing there. Keeping the record in the SVG means the evidence travels with the file it vouches for. Refusing a setting follows the brief's *Taste* and keeps the slice a PATCH. Byte comparison for the text files can never skip wrongly, and it makes the run's output say what it actually did.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** the plan finds a second comment before the root element breaks a reader of these SVGs (a forge's image view, or the page's inlining). The key then moves into the one existing line's hash input, and `check.ts`'s comparison changes with it.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (User Story 4 scenario 1 reworded, and S11's criteria for rules 1 to 10); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (S11's row: three diagrams, a frame-count-preserving edit)
- **Status:** standing

## D69 — What does SC-004's *one browser, under 2 seconds* measure, on which model, and what holds it?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S11-render-once · **When:** 2026-10-03T21:48:41Z · **Iteration:** 10
- **Question:** SC-004 says `make model` on a 16-slice model with one changed slice launches one browser and finishes in under 2 seconds. Unstated: (1) what the 16-slice model and *one changed slice* are; (2) what the 2 seconds times, on which tree and machine, and whether a test or a demo measurement holds it; (3) whether a run with nothing to draw launches a browser, and how the count is held; (4) what the run prints once diagrams can be left; (5) whether a first run, with nothing to skip, carries a time bound.
- **Options:** (a) hold only the counts in tests through a fake renderer (sessions opened, diagrams drawn, diagrams left), and state the 2 seconds as a demo measurement on the reference fixture, written in the quickstart with the machine and the command, timing the whole `make model` on a warm tree — the stage's recommendation; (b) a timing assertion in the suite; (c) drop the time and keep the counts.
- **Decision:** (a), the stage's recommendation, with these readings.
  - **(1) The fixture.** The reference fixture is a project generated with the `event-modelling` profile, Python backend, no frontend, target `none`. Its model holds 16 state-change slices of three frames each (ui, cmd, evt) under the default `render` settings, with no slice reading another. That gives 25 diagrams: 1 global, 8 segments, 16 slice diagrams. *One changed slice* is an edit to one slice's own content that no other slice reads, for example a frame's name. The number of diagrams that edit redraws is D67's and is taken from there.
  - **(2) The clock.** The 2 seconds is the wall time of the whole `make model` recipe as the developer types it, including its no-op install step. It is taken on a warm tree: mermaid-cli installed, and every diagram drawn by a previous run of the same model before the one edit. No PNG. It is measured at the demo on the machine the demo runs on, and written into the quickstart with the command, the machine and the number, as D47's count is said. It is not a timing assertion in the factory's suite. `MERMAID_PUPPETEER_CONFIG` is set where Chromium cannot sandbox, and the quickstart says so. A demo measuring 2 seconds or more is a failed demo, not a revised number.
  - **(3) The count.** A run opens at most one renderer session: exactly one when at least one diagram is to be drawn, none when there is nothing to draw. SC-004's *one browser* is the changed-slice case. The suite holds this at a seam in `render.ts`: the session is opened lazily, on the first diagram to draw, and a fake written in the test tree implements the same interface and records sessions opened and diagrams drawn. The tests cover the changed-slice run, the nothing-changed run and the first run. No mocking framework, no browser. The demo validates the fake: the hand confirms one real browser launch by a means other than the run's own closing line.
  - **(4) What it prints.** One `wrote <path>` line for each file the run wrote, as today, and no line for a file it left. The closing line keeps its `model:` opening and its pointer to the page, and carries the counts. On the fixture: `model: 16 slices, 3 of 25 diagrams drawn, 22 unchanged. Open docs/event-model/model.html to browse it.` (the 3 and 22 follow D67). When nothing changed: `model: 16 slices, 0 of 25 diagrams drawn, 25 unchanged; no browser started. Open docs/event-model/model.html to browse it.` The empty-model message stays as it is.
  - **(5) A first run.** On a fresh checkout or in CI, only the counts are claimed: one session, 25 drawn, 0 unchanged. No time bound. The quickstart records the first-run time beside today's 15.95 s as a before-and-after, for information only.
  - **Unavailable:** nothing is decided on a missing fact. The under-2-seconds figure itself is unmeasured until the demo; the estimate from this iteration's numbers is in *Why*.
- **Why:** The developer types `make model`, so the recipe is what they wait on, and timing the script alone would claim 0.3 s they still pay. The warm tree is the case the story is about: a slice changed in a model already drawn, which today costs 25 browsers and about 16 seconds. A wall-clock assertion cannot live in the suite. The factory's CI has no browser, the constitution's deterministic-suite rule excludes wall-clock dependence, and owner priority 5 puts deterministic over fast: a timing test would be either flaky or too loose to mean anything. The counts are the deterministic property behind the time, since each launch measured about 0.6 s; the suite holds the cause and the demo measures the effect. Dropping the time (c) would rewrite a success criterion the owner wrote, and it looks reachable: about 0.3 s of install, the script's start-up and one session drawing three small diagrams. No browser when nothing changed follows priority 2, fewer runs of the same work on the same content. Leaving `wrote` to mean written keeps the word true, and the closing line is the owner's *Taste*: one line saying what the run did. Nothing in the factory's tests or docs reads the present closing line verbatim (searched `src`, `tests`, `assets`, `docs` for `slices rendered`), so changing its words breaks no reader. A first run gets no bound because its time is 25 drawings on whatever machine CI is; the count is the promise there.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** the demo's warm changed-slice run, through one session, measures 2 seconds or more on the reference fixture for a reason the slice cannot remove (Chromium start-up plus the recipe's fixed cost). The bound then goes back to a person as a change to SC-004, and the counts stand.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (SC-004, User Story 4's Independent Test and first acceptance scenario, S11's criteria); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (S11's row); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/slices/S11-render-once/quickstart.md` (the demo's measurement, with machine and command)
- **Status:** standing

## D70 — Does the browser join the renderer key, and how far?
- **Stage:** convergence (pass 1) · **Slice:** S11-render-once · **When:** 2026-10-03T22:32:30Z · **Iteration:** 10
- **Question:** The key holds the installed mermaid-cli and mermaid versions, the drawing scripts' bytes and the Puppeteer config's bytes, but not Puppeteer or the browser. A prefix reinstalled with a different Puppeteer, or a browser upgraded behind the `executablePath` a config names, leaves every SVG *current*. The owner brief's priority 5 says a cache's key includes tool versions. Does the browser join the key, and how far?
- **Options:** (a) leave D68's set as it is; (b) add the installed `puppeteer` version, read from its `package.json` under the prefix as the other two are, with a browser named by `executablePath` covered only as far as the config file's bytes — **the stage's recommendation**; (c) also ask the launched browser for its version, at the cost of a launch on every run or a second record.
- **Decision:** (b), the stage's recommendation. This extends D68's rule 2 by one input and overrides nothing else in D68.
  1. **The key gains one part.** It is the `version` in `scripts/event-model/.mermaid-cli/node_modules/puppeteer/package.json`, the same package root `entryOf('puppeteer')` loads the launcher from. It is what is installed, not a constant, and is length-prefixed like the other parts.
  2. **Its place.** It goes after the mermaid version and before the script bytes. D68's "closed set" of the key is now: mermaid-cli version, mermaid version, puppeteer version, the drawing scripts' bytes, the config's bytes or the fixed word.
  3. **A missing or unreadable manifest** fails the same way as the mermaid-cli one. There is no fallback word: a prefix without Puppeteer cannot draw anyway.
  4. **A browser named by `executablePath`** is covered only as far as the config file's bytes. The README sentence on forcing a redraw (D68 rule 7) gains one clause: a browser upgraded behind a path the config names is not noticed, and deleting `segments/` and `slices/` is the remedy.
  5. **(c) is refused.** It needs a launch when nothing is drawn, which AC-S11-1 and D69 (3) forbid, or a second record, which D68 rule 3 refused as a manifest.
  6. **Unavailable:** whether two Chrome builds draw different bytes for the same Mermaid source. Nobody here has measured it, and the decision does not turn on it. The tool was swapped, so the key says so, whatever the bytes would have been.
  7. **Level:** PATCH, unchanged; `VERSION` stays `1.6.0.dev0`. There is no new file, setting, flag or ignore line.
  8. **Catch-up:** unchanged. A generated repository is still asked to do nothing after `slipwai migrate`. The existing fragment's line naming what the key covers gains the word Puppeteer; no second fragment.
  9. **One-time effect on a tree drawn by this slice's earlier commits:** every SVG carries a key computed without the Puppeteer version, so the next `make model` redraws all of them once (25 of 25 on the reference fixture, one browser). None of those commits was released, so only this branch's working trees and the converge fixture under `/tmp/s11-converge/fx` see it. A tree drawn by a released factory has no renderer line and redraws once under D68 rule 4 either way.
- **Why:** The developer reads these pictures to decide what a slice touches, and D68 already settled that a picture reported current that is not is worse than a slow one. Priority 5 names tool versions as part of the key. Puppeteer is a tool the drawing runs through: `render-session.ts` imports it from the prefix and launches the Chrome build that version downloads. Leaving it out (a) is the one gap in a set the brief's own words close. The cost is one line and one more read of a file already on disk, and a redraw only when the tool really changed, which is rare and takes one browser. Asking the browser itself (c) would break the promise the developer most often meets, that a run with nothing to draw starts no browser. The `executablePath` case is one the developer set up by hand, in a file whose bytes are already in the key, so a sentence in the README is the honest limit rather than a mechanism.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** high · **Would reverse if:** the prefix stops carrying a `puppeteer` package of its own (mermaid-cli moves to `puppeteer-core` alone or another launcher). The key then reads the version of whatever package `render-session.ts` launches through.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (AC-S11-6: the installed puppeteer version joins the list); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/slices/S11-render-once/plan.md` (the key's closed set); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/slices/S11-render-once/tasks.md` (the converge task this lead becomes); `assets/toolkit/scripts/event-model/render-session.ts` (the renderer key and its comment); `assets/toolkit/docs/event-model/README.md` (the clause on a browser path the config names); `changelog.d/render-once.md` (Puppeteer named in the key)
- **Status:** standing

## D73 — What is *the tree* a verify stamp vouches for, and what joins its key beyond FR-001's three, so that a reused stamp never stands for a gate that would fail if it ran now?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S03-verify-stamp · **When:** 2026-10-04T01:42:22Z · **Iteration:** 11
- **Question:** FR-001 keys the stamp by git tree hash, gate-script hash and recorded tool versions. `make verify` judges working files, not a commit. Some checks answer from things no tree hash holds: history and position (`check-slice-scope`, `check-migrations`, `check-flags`), files git ignores (`.codegraph/`, the harness projections, an installed extension kit), and environment variables (`UX_GATES_REQUIRE`, `UX_GATES_SINCE`, `UX_GATES_SHARD`, `CODEGRAPH_GATE_NO_SYNC`, `SLIPWAI_NO_INSTALL`). What is the tree, what else is in the key, what is deliberately left out, and what does the gate do when it cannot tell?
- **Options:** (a) FR-001's letter: `HEAD`'s tree, script hash, tool versions, stamped only on a clean working tree; (b) the working files as git would add them, plus the `HEAD` commit, branch name and trunk commit, plus a hash of the ignored inputs a check reads, variables in the key or the run unstamped, and the full gate with no stamp where the key cannot be built — **the stage's recommendation**; (c) as (b), but a tree with uncommitted changes is never stamped; (d) another line.
- **Decision:** (b), the stage's recommendation, strengthened in four places (rules 1, 2, 3 and 8). Each numbered rule is one a test can hold.
  1. **The tree is raw bytes.** The key covers every file git tracks and every untracked file git does not ignore, as they are on disk now.
     - For each file it covers the path, the raw bytes, the executable bit, and a symlink's target.
     - The bytes are read on every run. A file is never taken as unchanged on git's stat cache, its modification time, or git's own comparison.
     - This departs from "as `git add -A` would see it", because D49 found that comparison normalises changes away. Two examples are owed: a file rewritten with CRLF under `* text=auto eol=lf`, and a same-size rewrite with its modification time restored. Each must run the full gate.
     - The real index is never written.
  2. **What is tracked joins the key.** The key holds the real index's entries (names, modes, blob ids, stages). A file that went from untracked to tracked has the same bytes, but `check-codegraph` answers differently for it.
  3. **History and position.** The key holds four things:
     - what `HEAD` is: the branch name, or detached;
     - the commit `HEAD` names;
     - every ref under `refs/heads` and `refs/remotes` with the commit it names;
     - whether the checkout is shallow, and its boundary.
     
     This is wider than the stage's "the trunk ref". `check-migrations` and `check-flags` each try `main`, `origin/main`, `master` and `origin/master`, and `check-slice-scope` reads `ci.branch` and whether `origin` exists. All refs is the one set that cannot fall behind them.
  4. **Gate scripts.** FR-001's gate-script hash stays as a named part of the key. It is redundant under rule 1 wherever the scripts sit inside the covered files; it costs one hash and keeps FR-001's words and scenario 4 testable on their own. It covers the `Makefile` the gate ran from and everything under the project's `scripts/`.
  5. **Ignored inputs, in one closed list beside the checks.** Each entry names a path git ignores and the check that reads it. The key covers each by its bytes, or by the installed manifest that pins it where it is an installed dependency tree (D68's "what is installed, not the constant"). Absence is a value, so an extension installed after the stamp moves the key.
     - The list holds at least: the code index's database and its write-ahead file; every harness projection directory the registry names; the installed UX-gates kit; the installed `skills/ui-ux-pro-max/`; and `.env`, where a gate step reads it.
     - A test holds the list against the checks. A check that reads an ignored path not on the list fails that test.
  6. **Installed environments.** `.venv` and `node_modules` are outside the key only where the gate's own recipe rebuilds them from a committed lock on every run. The lock is in rule 1. Where a backend's recipe does not rebuild one, it is a rule 5 entry, covered by its installed manifest. The plan settles this per backend.
  7. **Variables.** Every environment variable a check reads that can change an answer joins the key by value, with unset distinct from empty.
     - At least: `UX_GATES_REQUIRE`, `UX_GATES_SINCE`, `UX_GATES_SHARD`, `CODEGRAPH_GATE_NO_SYNC`, `SLIPWAI_NO_INSTALL`, `GITHUB_HEAD_REF`, `CI_COMMIT_REF_NAME`.
     - `UX_GATES_JOBS` is not in the key: it changes how many run at once, never what is answered.
     - A run that makes the gate write the tree it judges (`RATCHET_TIGHTEN`) neither reads nor writes a stamp.
     - The same closed-list test as rule 5 holds the variables.
  8. **Written only by a pass that ran everything.** A stamp is written only by a run in which every prerequisite of `verify` ran and exited 0.
     - A run under make's ignore-errors, dry-run, touch or question modes neither reads nor writes one. `make -i verify` on a failing tree must leave no stamp.
     - The key is computed before the checks start and again after they pass. The stamp is written only where the two are equal, so a file edited or an index repaired during the run is never vouched for.
     - A stamp is never written after a failure, a skip or a reuse.
  9. **Never stamped.** A checkout is unstamped where any index entry is marked `assume-unchanged` or `skip-worktree` (D46 rule 4), where any entry is a submodule, or where an untracked directory is itself a repository. The key cannot see inside any of these.
  10. **Cannot tell.** The full gate runs, no stamp is read or written, and one line says why. The run never fails for that reason alone and never passes on less. This covers:
      - no git on `PATH`, not a repository, or git failing;
      - a file in the key that cannot be read;
      - an unreadable or malformed stamp;
      - a stamp whose key differs in any part.
  11. **(a) and (c) are refused.** A developer and every delegate run the gate on uncommitted work. A clean-tree-only stamp would vouch by a commit's name for files the gate never judged in that state, and would remove few of the repeated runs the story counts.
  - **Deliberately not in the key, and why none caches a false green on the tree's own content:**
    - Tool caches (`.ruff_cache`, `.pytest_cache`, `__pycache__`, `target/`): the full gate reads the same caches, so a stamp is no weaker than a run.
    - The stamp file itself: it depends on D76 placing it where git ignores it.
    - Tool versions: D75's.
  - **Residual for the cruise report:** what a project's own tests or tools read from outside the repository is not seen by the key. That means the clock, the network, user-level tool configuration, `PATH`, and variables no gate script names. A gate that would now fail for one of those reasons alone is reused as green until any file, ref or listed input moves, or `VERIFY_FORCE` is given.
  - **Dependencies on siblings, not decided here:** the residual's catch is the full gate in CI and at the merge root, which depends on D74 keeping stamps unread there. Rule 10's line and the stamp's location are D76's wording and place. Whether rule 1's read of every file fits SC-001 on a large repository is D75's measurement.
  - **No constitution MUST is broken and no person is needed.** The stamp is additive and nothing CI or the merge root checks changes, given D74.
- **Why:** The developer sees two things: how long a slice takes, and whether a green gate can be believed. A stamp that says *passed* for a tree the gate would now fail is the one outcome the owner brief calls pointless, and priority 5 says such a cache is wrong. The key therefore holds everything a check answers from, not a commit's name.
  - **Raw bytes are affordable.** Run by the skipper on this repository, read-only: hashing every tracked and untracked-not-ignored file with filters off took 60 ms, and hashing the index's entries took 2 ms. D49's stat-based residual therefore need not be accepted here at all.
  - **The refs were read, not run.** `check-migrations.py` and the aws `check-flags.py` each walk four candidate trunk names, in different orders. A key holding one trunk commit would miss a fetch that moves `origin/main`.
  - **The ignored inputs were read, not run.** `catalog.json` gives the three extension ignore lines; `src/slipwai/project/gitignore.py` gives the projection directories; `check-ux-gates.py` and `check-codegraph.py` give the five variables.
  - **Not verified:** that every backend's verify script re-syncs its environment on each run, which is why rule 6 is conditional; the size of a real code index database, since this checkout has none; Windows.
  - **One cost the host should place.** The files a run writes under `specs/` between gate runs (the decision log, the benchmark records, the untracked `specs/cruise-log.jsonl` here) are inputs of `check-decisions` and `check-benchmark`. They move the key honestly, so a cruise run reuses fewer stamps than the story's *two of three*. Narrowing that is per-check stamps (S07), not a hole to cut in this key.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** D75's measurement shows reading every covered file cannot meet SC-001 on the reference project. Rule 1 then falls back to D49's record (size, modification time, change time and identity per file, hashed where any differs) with D49's residual named, and every other rule stands.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (S03's criteria for rules 1 to 11, the Verify stamp entity's fields, and the residual); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (S03's row: the CRLF, restored-mtime, moved-ref, ignore-errors and newly-tracked examples)
- **Status:** standing

## D74 — Where is a verify stamp never read, and may an adopted repository's gate stamp at all?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S03-verify-stamp · **When:** 2026-10-04T01:42:22Z · **Iteration:** 11
- **Question:** The row says *Given `CI=true`, then no stamp is read* and owner priority 5 says a stamp is never trusted in CI. Three things it leaves open. (1) Which runs count as CI, and does a CI run write a stamp nobody reads? (2) Does the trunk reuse a stamp, given the constitution's *the merge root and CI run the full gate on every change* and the owner's *Always ask a person* on anything that changes what the merge root checks? (3) Does `make ci` reuse a stamp for its `verify` part, and does the gate of a repository that adopted the method stamp at all?
- **Options:**
  - (1) `CI=true` alone; or D32's list (`CI`, `GITHUB_ACTIONS`, `GITLAB_CI` non-empty).
  - (2a) Only CI markers stop a read, so the trunk reuses a stamp like any branch.
  - (2b) No read on the trunk (D30's name), under a CI marker, or on a detached `HEAD`; every other branch reads. **Recommended by the stage.**
  - (2c) D46's line: read only on `slice/<id>`.
  - (2d) Another line.
  - (3, `make ci`) Reuse the stamp; or always run `verify` in full. **The stage recommends always run.**
  - (3, adopted gate) Stamp under the same rule; stamp only the factory's own checks; or never stamp. No stage recommendation.
- **Decision:** (1) D32's list; (2b), the stage's recommendation, taken; `make ci` always runs in full, the stage's recommendation, taken; the adopted gate never stamps in this slice. As rules a test can hold:
  - **R1, CI.** A run is CI when any of `CI`, `GITHUB_ACTIONS` or `GITLAB_CI` is non-empty. This is D32's list, read the way `check-codegraph.py` and `check-slice-scope.py` read it, so `CI=false` counts as CI. Such a run reads no stamp and runs every prerequisite of `verify`.
  - **R2, the trunk.** A run reads no stamp when the checked-out branch is the trunk as D30 and D33 resolve it: a usable `ci.branch` from `project.json` that has a ref, else `main` where it has a ref, else `master`. The tests reuse D30's resolution and do not redefine it.
  - **R3, detached `HEAD`.** A run on a detached `HEAD` reads no stamp.
  - **R4, every other branch reads.** `slice/<id>`, a topic branch and this run's `adopt-method` (D12) read a stamp when the key matches.
  - **R5, no read means no write.** A run that R1, R2 or R3 stops from reading writes no stamp and leaves any existing stamp file untouched. After a green gate in CI or on the trunk, the tree holds no stamp that run wrote.
  - **R6, `make ci`.** `make ci` on a stamped tree runs every prerequisite of `verify`, on any branch. It is a forced run; what a forced run writes is D76's to say, and R5 still holds where R1 to R3 apply.
  - **R7, the adopted gate.** Where the project has a wrapped application (the case in which `makefile()` adds `check-convergence`), the generated `verify` rule neither reads nor writes a stamp. Two consecutive runs there both run every prerequisite. `NOTHING_CONFIRMED` is unchanged.
  - **R8, later merge nodes.** Any slice that makes a branch other than the trunk a place slices merge into (the merge tree) adds that branch shape to R2 in the same slice.
  - **Does the trunk need a person?** No. Under R2 the merge root and CI run exactly what they run today, so nothing they check changes. Option (2a), reading a stamp on the trunk, is **not available to this run**: D46 already read the constitution's MUST as forbidding a narrowed local trunk run, and the owner's *Always ask a person* covers it. It stays a person's call.
  - **What the host must also change.** User Story 1's Independent Test and acceptance scenario 1 need the words *on a branch other than the trunk, with no CI marker set*. A generated project starts on `main`, where the second run is now a full run by design. SC-001 is measured on such a branch.
  - **Dependence on D73.** If the key carries the branch name, a stamp never crosses branches and R5 costs nothing. If it does not, R5 is what stops a trunk run from vouching for a branch.
- **Why:** The developer sees two things: how long a slice takes, and whether the gate can still be believed.
  - *Speed is where the repeats are.* User Story 1's repeated runs happen on the branch the slice is built on, and R4 gives them back there, for a `/drive` slice and for a developer on a `fix/…` branch alike. Option (2c) would withhold that from every topic branch and from this run for no gain in safety.
  - *Why R4 is wider than D46.* D46 narrowed a check over changed content, which can miss something. A stamp is written only by a full passing gate and matches only byte-identical content, so reuse on a branch checks nothing less.
  - *Why the trunk is excluded anyway.* Whether a reused stamp counts as "the merge root ran the full gate" is arguable, and an arguable reading of a NON-NEGOTIABLE MUST is not one the run takes. FR-023 already writes the shape: *always on `main` and in CI*.
  - *The cost on `adopt-method`.* While the adoption is unmerged, this branch is where slices land and it will read a stamp. That is accepted: every change there still gets a full run, because no stamp can exist for content that has not passed one. R8 keeps the merge tree from inheriting the gap silently.
  - *`make ci`.* It is documented as the local equivalent of the extended CI gate. A stand-in for CI that trusts a stamp is not one (priority 5).
  - *The adopted gate.* Its `lint`, `typecheck` and `test` are commands a person recorded for code the factory did not write. They are held to nothing the architecture rules promise, so they may read a database, a sibling directory or an ignored file. A whole-gate stamp keyed on the tree could then cache a false green, which priority 5 calls wrong.
  - *Why not the middle option.* Stamping only the factory's own checks is a per-check stamp, which the row sends to S07.
  - *Why never-stamp is cheap.* Priority 3 puts the generated project first, the brownfield path is experimental so it may differ, adding the stamp later is additive, and this repository takes nothing until a person runs `migrate` (D9).
  - Not verified: no reproduction was run, the PRD artifact was not consulted, and that a `make` variable set on `ci` reaches the `verify` prerequisite under GNU Make 3.81 is from the skipper's memory. The mechanism is the plan's.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** a person says in writing that a reused stamp on the trunk is the full gate having run (R2 then drops and SC-002 can be met by the stamp alone), or that an adopted repository's recorded commands may be treated as reading only the tree (R7 then drops). Either is theirs alone to say.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (User Story 1's Independent Test and scenario 1 qualified, and the criteria for R1 to R8, numbered by the host); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (the S03 row's last example widened to R1 to R3, and a Parking Lot line for R8 and for per-check stamps in the adopted gate under S07)
- **Status:** standing

## D75 — Which tools' versions join the verify stamp's key, how are they read, what does *recorded* mean, and what does SC-001's *under 1 second* measure, on what, held by what?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S03-verify-stamp · **When:** 2026-10-04T01:42:22Z · **Iteration:** 11
- **Question:** FR-001 keys the stamp by *recorded tool versions* and the row's fourth example names *a new ruff version*. Unstated: which tools, how each version is read, and what *recorded* means. SC-001 gives the reuse run one second; unstated: what the second times, on which project, and whether a test or a demo holds it. Asking uv, python3, node, git and make together took 0.10 s on this machine; a JVM takes a few tenths.
- **Options:** (a) ask every tool every recipe launches, in-environment ones included, on every run; (b) the key holds what each machine-supplied tool reports, from one table beside the backend tooling table plus make and git, with lock-pinned tools covered by the tree hash and the installed environment's record, and a tool that cannot be asked meaning no stamp is read or written — **the stage's recommendation**; (c) no launches, each tool identified by its executable's resolved path, size and modification time; (d) another line. For SC-001 the stage recommends the wall time of `make verify` on a stamped, unchanged generated Python skeleton, measured at the demo, with the suite holding the mechanism rather than a clock.
- **Decision:** (b), the stage's recommendation, with one departure in rule 3; and the stage's reading of SC-001.
  1. **The set.** The key's tool versions are those of the tools the machine supplies and no committed file pins.
     - Every project: `make`, `git` and the `python3` on PATH that runs the gate's check scripts.
     - Python backend: `uv`.
     - TypeScript backend, or any project with a frontend: `node` and `npm`.
     - Go backend: `go`.
     - Both Java backends: `java` only; Maven comes through the committed `./mvnw` wrapper, whose pin is a tracked file.
     - A project with several backends takes the union, each tool asked once per run.
  2. **One table.** The set lives in one table beside `BACKEND_TOOLING` in `src/slipwai/backends.py`, keyed by the same backend names. A test fails when a key of `BACKEND_TOOLING` has no row, so a new backend cannot ship without saying what its machine supplies.
  3. **Lock-pinned tools are not asked and not read.** This covers ruff, mypy, pytest, staticcheck through `go tool`, the Maven plugins and everything under `package-lock.json`. Their versions move only when a tracked file moves, and the tree hash holds that. The row's *new ruff version* is therefore a changed `uv.lock` or `pyproject.toml`, and the example is tested that way. Here I depart from the stage: the installed ruff is not read even where it costs no launch. One thing is read from the environment without a launch: the interpreter version in a Python service's `.venv/pyvenv.cfg`, because no lock pins that patch level and it is what mypy and pytest run under.
  4. **How a version is read.** On every run that would read or write a stamp, each tool in the set is launched once with its version argument. The key takes the first non-empty line it prints, whole and unparsed, so a vendor or build change moves the key as a number change does. Launches may run concurrently. No path, size or modification time of an executable enters the key or the stamp.
  5. **A tool that cannot be asked.** If a tool is not on PATH, exits non-zero, prints nothing or does not answer within a fixed timeout, or a `pyvenv.cfg` that rule 3 names is missing or unreadable, the run reads no stamp and runs the full gate. A stamp is written only if every part can be read after the gate passed. The run never fails for this reason alone and never passes on a partial key. Whether it says so, and in what words, belongs with the reuse line (D76).
  6. **Recorded.** The stamp holds, beside the key, each tool's name and the line it reported, as plain text a person can read. A stamp whose recorded versions do not all equal this run's is not reused.
  7. **SC-001's second.** It is the wall time of the whole `make verify` recipe as the developer types it. The project is one generated with the Python backend, no frontend and target `none` (the Independent Test's project). The tree has just passed the full gate and is unchanged, and the run is local with no CI marker. It is measured at the demo on the demo's machine and written into the slice's quickstart with the command, the machine and the number, as D69 did for SC-004. A demo measuring one second or more is a failed demo, not a revised number.
  8. **What the suite holds.** There is no timing assertion. Stand-in executables written in the test tree are put on PATH and record every call. On a reuse run the only processes started are the version questions of rule 1; no sync, no check script, no linter, type checker or test runner starts. The suite also holds that a changed line from any one tool, and a changed lock, each cause the full gate to run and a new stamp to be written.
  9. **Other backends.** They carry the same mechanism and no time claim. The quickstart names the Java backends as the residual: a JVM's start-up is inside the budget on this machine by estimate only. No backend's toolchain is dropped from the key to meet the second.
  10. **(c) is not available as written.** A stamp holding resolved executable paths breaks the constitution's rule that persisted data carries no file paths. It is also wrong on this machine: `uv` resolves to `/snap/bin/uv`, a launcher whose path does not change when the tool behind it does.
  11. **Unavailable:** nothing is decided on a missing fact. The reuse run's time is unmeasured until the demo. Not verified here: the exact name of the version line uv writes into `pyvenv.cfg`, and that `uv sync --locked` restores a hand-altered environment exactly; the plan confirms both before relying on them.
  12. **Dependency:** this rests on D73's tree hash covering the working files, locks and wrapper pins included; if D73 covers only the committed tree, a lock or pin with uncommitted changes must make the stamp unusable.
- **Why:** The developer wants two of three gate runs back, and must still be able to believe a green. A stamp says *this tree passed the gate*, and the gate's first act is to sync each environment to its lock. So the verdict a full run would give depends on the tree and on the tools the machine supplies, not on what happens to sit in `.venv` between runs. Asking in-environment tools (a) costs a launch each through `uv run` on every reuse run and spends the second on answers the tree hash already gives. Reading the installed ruff would invalidate stamps for a state the next full run erases. The interpreter is different: it is machine-supplied, uv keeps a compatible environment as it is, and a new patch release can change what mypy or pytest say, so priority 5 puts it in the key at the cost of one file read. Taking the whole reported line rather than a parsed number errs towards re-running. Never reading or writing a stamp on a partial key is the same priority: a stamp that could cache a false green is wrong. For SC-001, the recipe is what the developer waits on. A clock in the suite would be flaky or too loose to mean anything, as D69 found, and the constitution's deterministic-suite rule excludes it. The count of processes started is the deterministic cause; the demo measures the effect. Nothing is added to the project's dependencies and no setting is introduced.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** a full gate run can pass or fail differently on the same tree because of a tool this set leaves out — a sync that does not restore a hand-changed environment to the lock, or a machine-supplied tool the gate launches that the table does not name. That tool then joins the key by the cheapest read that is true, and the second is re-measured.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (FR-001, SC-001, User Story 1's Independent Test, the first Edge Case, the Verify stamp entity, S03's criteria); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (S03's row, the fourth example read as a changed lock); the slice's plan and quickstart when they are written (the table, the demo's measurement with machine and command)
- **Status:** standing

## D76 — What does a person see and control on a stamped gate: the reuse line, `VERIFY_FORCE`, a failed gate, a stamp's age, and where the stamp is kept?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S03-verify-stamp · **When:** 2026-10-04T01:42:22Z · **Iteration:** 11
- **Question:** S03's row says a reuse run *prints the stamp it reused and exits 0* and that `VERIFY_FORCE=1` runs the full gate. Unstated: (1) what the one line says, and what a full run that could reuse nothing says; (2) which values of `VERIFY_FORCE` force, and whether a forced pass writes a stamp; (3) what a failing gate does to a stamp; (4) whether a stamp expires; (5) where the stamp lives, how many are kept, and whether worktrees share one.
- **Options:**
  - *Line:* one plain line carrying that the gate did not run, that this tree passed, when, an abbreviated key and how to run it anyway; on a full run, one extra line only where the reason is one a person can act on — **the stage's recommendation**.
  - *Force:* only `1`; or any non-empty value other than `0`. A forced pass writes a stamp — **recommended**.
  - *Failure:* a failing run writes nothing and removes the stamp for the key it failed on — **recommended**.
  - *Age:* (a) no expiry, the pass's instant on the line — **recommended**; (b) a fixed bound; (c) a setting.
  - *Where:* an ignored file in the working tree; or the git directory, one stamp per worktree, no setting, not shared between worktrees — **recommended**.
- **Decision:** The stage's recommendation on all five, with rule 3 made stricter and four additions. Each rule is one a test can hold.
  1. **The reuse line.**
     - A reuse run prints exactly one line of its own on standard output, runs no check, and exits 0.
     - The line begins `verify:` and carries five facts:
       - the full gate did not run;
       - this tree already passed it;
       - the instant of that pass, read from the stamp, in UTC to the second;
       - an abbreviation of the stamp's key, at least seven hexadecimal characters;
       - the words `VERIFY_FORCE=1` as the way to run the gate anyway.
     - A reuse run never prints `verify: all gates passed`. That line stays byte for byte the closing line of a full passing run and of nothing else.
  2. **What a full run adds.** Nothing where there was no stamp or the key differs: that is the ordinary case. Exactly one line, before the first check, where:
     - the run was forced — the line names `VERIFY_FORCE` and its value;
     - git is absent or cannot read this checkout — the line carries git's reason;
     - a tool whose version the key needs cannot be asked — the line names the tool.

     In the last two cases the full gate runs and no stamp is written.
  3. **When a pass is not recorded.** A full run that passed but could not write its stamp, or found the key after the last check different from the key before the first, still exits 0 and prints the closing line. It adds one line saying the pass was not recorded and why. A stamp never changes the gate's exit code.
  4. **`VERIFY_FORCE`.**
     - Unset, empty or `0` does not force. Any other value forces.
     - It is read from the make command line or the environment.
     - A forced run reads no stamp and runs every check.
     - A forced passing run writes a stamp like any other full pass.
     - The page a project gets that describes the gate carries its default (unset) and one sentence on when to set it: when you want the checks run again on a tree that already passed.
  5. **A gate that does not pass.**
     - Every full run removes this project's stamp before its first check starts.
     - A stamp is written only after the last check passed.
     - So after a run that failed, was interrupted or was killed, no stamp exists, for any key.
     - This is stricter than the recommendation and overrides it: make stops at the first failing prerequisite, so a rule that acts on failure has nowhere to run.
     - Where the stamp cannot be removed, the run prints one line naming the file to delete and writes no stamp.
  6. **Age.** (a). A stamp never expires and there is no setting. Its timestamp is shown, never compared. Deleting the stamp is always safe and has the same effect as forcing.
  7. **Where it lives.**
     - In a directory of the factory's own under the directory `git rev-parse --git-dir` answers for this checkout. It is never a file git itself owns and never anywhere in the working tree.
     - No ignore line is added. After any run, `git status` reports nothing the stamp left.
     - One stamp per project per worktree: the last passing key, replaced by a rename of a complete file.
     - Two projects in one repository (D36) never read each other's stamp.
     - A stamp path that is not a regular file is removed as itself, never read or written through (D72's rule).
     - A stamp that cannot be parsed, or lacks any of the entity's five fields, is no stamp.
  8. **Worktrees.** A stamp is never shared between worktrees of one repository at this slice. Sharing is a later slice's question, with the merge tree.
  9. **What it asks of an existing project.** Nothing. `slipwai migrate` brings the new Makefile, the first `make verify` runs in full, and the project's `.gitignore` is not touched. The fragment says so. The level stays MINOR as the row names it; the *new file the gate writes* is one a person never sees in `git status`.
  - **Depends on:** D73 for what the key is (rules 1 and 3 need it reduced to one digest), D74 for where no stamp is read or written (rules 2 to 5 apply only where D74 allows a stamp), and D75 for which tools are asked.
  - **Unavailable:** nothing.
- **Why:**
  - The developer has to believe a gate that returns in under a second. The line tells them it did not run, when the tree last passed, and how to make it run, which is the brief's *one line a person can act on*.
  - Keeping `all gates passed` for a real run means a log never claims checks that did not happen. Only tests and two `docs/` pages read that line today, so nothing breaks.
  - Treating every value but empty and `0` as force errs towards running the gate. The forced line makes a `VERIFY_FORCE` exported by mistake visible rather than a silent loss of the saving.
  - Removing the stamp before the run is the only form of the failure rule that holds under make and under a kill. It costs one full run after reverting to an earlier passing tree, which is the safe side of owner priority 5.
  - The second key check keeps an edit made mid-run from being vouched for by checks that read the older bytes.
  - An expiry would not make a date-reading test deterministic. It would only add a number nobody chose. CI and the merge root run in full and are where such a test is caught.
  - D46 kept its memory in `.codegraph/` because that directory was already there and already ignored. No such directory is guaranteed here, and a new ignore line would touch a file the project owns (constitution I). The git directory needs none.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** the plan finds a supported checkout where the gate cannot write under the git directory though git works there (a read-only or shared git directory in a container). The stamp then moves to an ignored path in the working tree under D46's rule — written only where git already ignores it — with a `migrate` catch-up note for the ignore line.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (S03's criteria for rules 1 to 9; User Story 1 scenario 1 and the Verify stamp entity's result field, which is only ever a pass); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (S03's row: the file is under the git directory, and a run that does not pass leaves no stamp)
- **Status:** standing

## D77 — Where D73, D74, D75 and D76 differ at their borders, which wording holds: which full runs say a line, what a run that may not read a stamp does to one, and whether a pass with a key it could not build beforehand is recorded?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S03-verify-stamp · **When:** 2026-10-04T01:58:00Z · **Iteration:** 11
- **Question:** Four delegates decided at once and none saw the others. (1) D73's rule 10 has *one line says why* for every case it cannot tell, a differing key and a malformed stamp among them; D76's rule 2 prints nothing where there is no stamp or the key differs, and a line in three named cases only. (2) D76's rule 5 removes the stamp before every full run; D74's R5 leaves any stamp untouched on the trunk, in CI and on a detached `HEAD`, and says nothing of what such a run prints. (3) D75's rule 5 writes a stamp where every part can be read after the gate passed; D76's rule 2 writes none where a tool could not be asked; D73's rule 8 writes only where the key before equals the key after. (4) D74's R6 makes `make ci` a forced run without saying what it prints.
- **Options:** (a) each entry's own words where it owns the matter, as the entries themselves assign it — D73 gives the line's wording and the stamp's place to D76, D76 applies its rules 2 to 5 only where D74 allows a stamp, D76's note gives the timing of the key to D73 — **the reading the entries recommend**; (b) send the four borders to a fifth delegate.
- **Decision:** (a). (1) A full run says one line before its first check only where a person can act on the reason: it was forced; git is absent or cannot read the checkout; a tool or a file the key needs cannot be asked or read; or the checkout is one D73's rule 9 never stamps, the line saying which. No stamp, a differing key, and a stamp that cannot be parsed are the ordinary case and say nothing. (2) On the trunk, under a CI marker and on a detached `HEAD`, a run reads nothing, writes nothing, removes nothing, and prints what it prints today and nothing more; D76's removal before the first check applies where a stamp may be used, and to a forced run there. (3) A run that could not build the whole key before its first check writes no stamp even if it passes, since there is no earlier key for D73's rule 8 to compare: the first gate of a fresh clone records nothing and the second does. (4) `make ci` prints the forced line and writes what a forced pass writes, except where (2) applies.
- **Why:** A developer reads one line and acts on it, so a line appears only where there is something to do, and the merge root and CI say exactly what they said before this slice, which is the plainest proof nothing they check changed. Losing one stamp on a fresh clone costs one full run and can never record a pass for files the checks did not all see.
- **Decided by:** host (standing decision D76)
- **Confidence:** high · **Would reverse if:** a person wants every unstamped run to say why, the trunk included; that is a line added, and no rule about when a stamp is read changes.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (S03's criteria on the lines a full run says, the trunk and CI, a fresh clone's first gate, and the extended gate)
- **Status:** standing

## D78 — Does a project the factory generated under a moved layout (the delivery material under `delivery/`, no wrapped application) take the stamped gate?
- **Stage:** implement · **Slice:** S03-verify-stamp · **When:** 2026-10-04T04:10:00Z · **Iteration:** 11
- **Question:** D74's R7 withholds the stamp *where the project has a wrapped application*. T013 found a third shape: a layout whose delivery material sits under `delivery/` with no application wrapped yet, where the stamped recipe as written cannot run (the script's path and the sub-make do not follow the layout). Stamp there, by teaching the recipe the layout, or leave that gate as it is?
- **Options:** (a) leave it as it is: the stamped rule is chosen only where no application is wrapped and the layout is not moved — **what the delegate built, and the reading R7's reason gives**; (b) carry the layout through the recipe so a moved layout with nothing wrapped stamps.
- **Decision:** (a). A moved layout is the layout of a repository that adopted the method, and R7's reason — commands a person recorded for code the factory did not write may read anything — holds for it as soon as one application is confirmed; a gate that stamped until then and stopped afterwards would change under the developer for no saving worth it. The `verify` rule there stays byte for byte today's.
- **Why:** The developer in an adopted repository sees one gate that always runs, whatever stage of adoption the repository is at; the experimental path may differ from the generated one (D74), and adding the stamp there later is additive.
- **Decided by:** host (standing decision D74)
- **Confidence:** high · **Would reverse if:** a person asks for stamps in adopted repositories (D74's own condition); the recipe then carries the layout.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (AC-S03-24, and what *where a stamp may be used* means)
- **Status:** standing

## D79 — When git cannot answer at the start of a run, the script cannot ask where the stamp is: does a stamp that stood before stay, or must the script find it another way?
- **Stage:** converge · **Slice:** S03-verify-stamp · **When:** 2026-10-04T05:25:00Z · **Iteration:** 11
- **Question:** AC-S03-26 says that after a run that failed no stamp exists for any key, and D76's rule 5 removes the stamp before the first check. Converge pass 1 (T019) showed one case where that cannot be done as built: git fails, so `git rev-parse` cannot say where the git directory is, the full gate runs (AC-S03-13), and a stamp written earlier stays where it was. If that gate fails and git later works, the next run can reuse the earlier stamp for the tree that did pass.
- **Options:** (a) leave it standing and say so in the criterion: the stamp vouches only for the key that passed, and a run with a working git compares every part of that key again — **the stage's recommendation (converge graded the case MEDIUM for that reason)**; (b) find the git directory without git, by walking up to a `.git` entry and reading a worktree's pointer file, and remove what is there.
- **Decision:** (a). Where git cannot answer, the run reads no stamp, writes none, and leaves one that stood; its one line carries git's reason (AC-S03-13). The stamp left is for a key every check passed on, and it is reused only where a later run, with git answering, builds the same key from the files, the index, the refs, the tools and the listed inputs as they then are.
- **Why:** A developer whose git is broken sees the full gate run and is told why nothing is recorded; nothing false is ever recorded. Reading git's own layout without git is a second, unowned reading of where a worktree keeps its files, and a wrong guess there removes or trusts the wrong file; the case it would close needs a gate that fails only while git is broken, on a tree that passed before and is unchanged after.
- **Decided by:** host (standing decision D76)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** a check is found whose failure with a broken git is a true failure of an unchanged tree that the same check passes with git working; the stamp's place is then found without git.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (AC-S03-13 and AC-S03-26)
- **Status:** standing

## D80 — What does converge pass 2 change in the rules: a run that tightens the ratchet (T024), and what of a tool's answer enters the key (T026)?
- **Stage:** converge · **Slice:** S03-verify-stamp · **When:** 2026-10-04T05:50:00Z · **Iteration:** 11
- **Question:** (1) T019's fix made a run with `RATCHET_TIGHTEN` set remove a standing stamp, against AC-S03-9 and D73's rule 7 (*neither reads nor writes a stamp*), and two examples of the slice's own suite went red: which behaviour stands? (2) D75's rule 4 takes *the first non-empty line a tool prints*; pass 2 showed a tool that prints a notice first hides a changed version on its second line, and the stamp is reused.
- **Options:** (1a) AC-S03-9 as written: a ratchet run touches nothing — **what the standing entry says**; (1b) a ratchet run removes the stamp as an ignore-errors run does. (2a) the first non-empty line, as D75 wrote it; (2b) the key takes everything the tool printed in answer to the version question, both streams, and the stamp shows a person the first non-empty line of its standard output, or of its standard error where it printed nothing there — **errs towards re-running, which is D75's own reason**.
- **Decision:** (1a) and (2b). A run with `RATCHET_TIGHTEN` set reads no stamp, writes none and removes none; the code returns to that and T019's example for it is corrected, while the ignore-errors mode keeps T019's removal. A tool's whole answer is in the key; a changed byte anywhere in it runs the gate. This amends D75's rule 4 in that one clause and overrides nothing else.
- **Why:** The ratchet belongs to the gate of a repository that adopted the method, which never stamps (D74, D78), so the rule is a guard and the plainest guard is to touch nothing; a stamp that stood is for a key that passed. For the tools, a developer must be able to believe a reused green after an upgrade, whatever a tool chooses to print before its version.
- **Decided by:** host (standing decision D73)
- **Confidence:** high · **Would reverse if:** a tool's version answer is found to vary from run to run with nothing changed (a timestamp, a random path), so that no stamp is ever reused on a machine; the key then takes the lines that name a version.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (AC-S03-15)
- **Status:** standing

## D81 — What does each open finding on S03 become before the demo (T021–T023, T027–T030, G1–G8): code now, a stated reading in the criterion, or a line for a later slice or the cruise report?
- **Stage:** gaps (after converge) · **Slice:** S03-verify-stamp · **When:** 2026-10-04T06:35:00Z · **Iteration:** 11
- **Question:** The slice converged after three passes with seven `LOW` tasks open (T021, T022, T023, T027, T028, T029, T030), and the after-converge gaps pass returned G1–G8, none above `MEDIUM`.
  - G1: an event-modelling project's gate launches `npm` and `node`, and the tool list asks them only for a TypeScript backend or a frontend.
  - G2: the Maven wrapper runs `JAVA_HOME`'s JVM where that is set, and the key asks the `java` on `PATH`.
  - G3: a trunk named neither `main` nor `master`, with no `ci.branch` recorded, reads and writes a stamp, and no page says how the trunk is recognised.
  - G4: on Windows `npm` is `npm.cmd`, which a launch without a shell is believed not to find; not run.
  - G5, G6: four quickstart steps are worded so the hand will misread them.
  - G7: what `S04-parallel-gate` inherits is written nowhere.
  - G8: two clauses of AC-S03-29 have no example that would fail.
  - Which become code before the demo, which a stated reading, which a line? Is G3's `ci.branch` a catch-up note, and does it move the level?
- **Options:**
  - (a) **The stage's recommendation, finding by finding.**
    - Code: G1, G2 (with `JAVA_HOME` keyed), G4, T021, T028 with G3.
    - A stated reading: T027, T029, T030, and G8's Make clause.
    - A line: G7, T022, T023, and G8's starters clause.
  - (b) (a) with departures wherever the constitution or a standing entry does not allow the recommendation.
  - (c) Park everything behind the PRD's slices.
- **Decision:** (b). I take the stage's recommendation except in four places: T030, G4, G2's keying and T022. No finding needs a person before the demo.
  1. **Code before the demo, each through a failing example first.**
     - **G1, taken.** The rule in AC-S03-14 becomes *every tool the machine supplies that a recipe of the gate launches*.
       - The model tooling's `node` and `npm` join the list wherever the gate has the model's checks, as a row of the one table, not a special case in the recipe.
       - A test holds it over the generated `Makefile` of every shape the scan already generates. The command each recipe line reachable from the gate launches must be on that project's tool list, a file of the project, or named in the test with the reason it is pinned.
       - This is D75's own reversal condition met (*a machine-supplied tool the gate launches that the table does not name*), so nothing is overridden.
     - **G2, taken in part.** The JVM asked is the one the wrapper would run: `JAVA_HOME`'s where that variable is non-empty, else the `java` on `PATH`.
       - Where `JAVA_HOME` is set and its JVM cannot be launched, that is AC-S03-17's case: no stamp, the full gate, one line.
       - **Departure:** `JAVA_HOME`'s value is not keyed. It is the path of an executable's directory, and AC-S03-15 keeps every such path out of the key and the stamp. The JVM's whole answer already moves the key when the JDK changes. A moved directory holding the same JDK is not a change the gate can see.
       - The closed-list test stops saying the wrapper and each backend's verify script *hold nothing*. Each shell script the scan cannot read is named with the variables that select which executable runs. Such a variable either decides which tool is asked or fails the test.
       - A variable that only configures a tool (`MAVEN_OPTS` and its kind) stays in AC-S03-31's residual, which the page already carries.
     - **T021, taken.** A line says what happened and what to do.
       - The moved-key line names which part of the key moved. Where it is the files, it says a check may have written one, that `git status` shows it, and that the next run records. It never implies an edit by the person.
       - No path is found by keeping a list of files between the two halves of a run: that would persist paths.
       - A stamp path that is a directory is called a directory.
       - T021's third item is G2's.
     - **T028 with G3, taken.** The page a project gets says three things, and the ships test holds each.
       - *When to force:* a check answers from one of the five things a stamp cannot see.
       - *How the trunk is recognised:* the branch `project.json` records as `ci.branch`, else `main`, else `master`.
       - *What a team with another trunk name does:* record `ci.branch`; until then that branch is treated as any other and reuses a stamp.
       - The page stops saying *everything the checks answer from*, and says *non-empty* where it said *set* of a CI marker.
     - **T030, departure: code, not a reading.** The constitution's persisted-data MUST forbids file paths, host names and process ids in what is stored. T030 shows the pattern letting a Windows path with a space, a `~user` path and a path after a comma through. A sentence in the criterion cannot make a MUST hold.
       - The stamp stores, per tool, its name, the version-shaped words of its answer (digits and dots with a short suffix; nothing else is admitted) and the digest of the whole answer. Where no such word is found, it stores the digest alone.
       - The key is unchanged: it still takes the whole answer (D80).
       - AC-S03-18 says what the field holds.
       - A test plants each of T030's lines and a host name, and finds none of them in any file under the stamp's directory.
  2. **A stated reading in the criterion, with one example each that pins what the code already does.**
     - **T029, taken.** A run that both tightens the ratchet and ignores errors touches nothing: the ratchet rule is asked first and wins (D80).
     - **T027, taken.** AC-S03-26 names, beside git that cannot answer, the two further runs that leave a stamp for a key that passed.
       - One is a run where the trunk cannot be resolved for a reason that is not git's.
       - The other is a run where no `python3` able to run the stamp script is on `PATH`.
       - Neither can tell whether it is on the trunk, where nothing may be removed (D74 R5). So neither removes anything.

  4. **Departure on T022: to the adversary, not to a later slice.** The host names *a project in a subdirectory of its repository* as a seam of this slice's adversary pass: a sibling's edit, committed and uncommitted, against every check that asks git a question.
     - Shown to move a verdict, it is fixed in this slice by D63's rule.

On an unrecorded trunk every new commit still moves the key and runs the full gate; only an unchanged tree is reused. The cruise report lists that for a person.

Recognising the trunk then needs a second source beside D30's, which changes what a merge root is taken to be and is theirs alone to say; item 6's catch-up line then goes.

## D82 — The owner's answer to D54: where is history fetched in CI, now that the fetch also gives `check-migrations` and `check-flags` a base?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S24-ci-fetches-slice-base · **When:** 2026-10-04T07:30:00Z · **Iteration:** 11
