- **Options:** (a) fetch full history on every run of the generated `verify` job — **the recommendation D54 recorded**; (b) fetch only on `slice/<id>` pull requests.
- **Decision:** (a), in the owner's words as queued through `/cruise-tell`: *Fetch full history on every run of the generated verify job (fetch-depth: 0; GIT_DEPTH 0 on GitLab) and accept that check-migrations and check-flags now hold their rules in CI on every pull request, as they already do on a developer's machine. This is inside the D39 approval. S24-ci-fetches-slice-base is unblocked: re-enter it at the open gaps note. Its changelog.d fragment must say plainly that check-migrations and check-flags now run in CI, so a maintainer who sees a red pull request after slipwai migrate knows why.*
- **Why:** The owner's: the two checks already hold their rules on a developer's machine, so CI holding them too is the same gate in both places; the fragment tells a maintainer why a pull request may turn red after `slipwai migrate`.
- **Decided by:** human
- **Confidence:** high · **Would reverse if:** the owner says otherwise.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (the note that opens S24's section; the run's checkpoint, a transient file, carried S24 as the next slice after S03, re-entered at slice gaps at its open note)
- **Status:** standing

## D83 — Where does each of the adversary's eighteen findings on S03 go, and how are A1, A2, C3 and C4 closed?
- **Stage:** Phase 4 (adversary) · **Slice:** S03-verify-stamp · **When:** 2026-10-04T08:40:00Z · **Iteration:** 11
- **Question:** Three seams attacked the slice at `ef66461` and returned eighteen findings plus one documented limit, none `CRITICAL`.
  - Seam A: A1 `HIGH` (an ignored file a check reads by walking the tree is in no part of the key), A2 `MEDIUM` (T022: a sibling's edit outside a project in a subdirectory), A3 `MEDIUM` (tags and replace refs), A4 `LOW` (the repository's git configuration).
  - Seam B: B1 `HIGH` (two runs at once stamp a failing tree), B2 `MEDIUM` (a hand-typed `record`), B3 `MEDIUM` and B4 `LOW` (whitespace stripped from git's paths), B5 `LOW` (wrong kind of file at the note's or the directory's path), B6 `LOW` (the stamp's instant printed as stored).
  - Seam C: C1 (an unborn `master` stamps), C2 `MEDIUM` (`$(MAKE)` unquoted), C3 `MEDIUM` (`ci` recognised by goals only), C4 `LOW` (an unusable `ci.branch` or unreadable `project.json` still stamps, silently), C5 and C6 `LOW`, C7 `LOW` (the adopted page describes a stamp), C8 `LOW` (three recipe cases).
  - The limit: a CI system that sets none of the three markers.
  - Which are fixed in this slice through a failing test, which stated, which a line? What are the rules for A1, A2, C3 and C4? Does C3's amendment of AC-S03-23 or A1's widened key need a person?
- **Options:**
  - (a) **The stage's recommendation.** Fix everything new with the slice that a test can show, under D63's rule: A1 by turning the list round, A2 by the whole repository, A3, A4, B1 with B2 by a run token, B3 with B4, B5, B6, C1, C2, C3 by `ci` depending on the checks' own target, C4, C5, C6, C7 and C8's `-f` case. State C8's doubled `lint`. Decline `MAKE=/bin/true`. One sentence and a report line for the unmarked CI systems. The older part of A3 goes to the Parking Lot.
  - (b) (a) with departures where the constitution, the brief or a standing entry asks for one.
  - (c) A defect slice behind the PRD's slices (D39).
- **Decision:** (b). I take the stage's recommendation on every finding's place, with six departures in the rules: items 1, 2, 5, 7, 8 and 14. No finding stays open past the slice, and no person is needed.
  1. **A1, fixed. The list is turned round (amends D73 rules 1, 5 and 6, and AC-S03-7 and AC-S03-8).**
     - The key covers every file under the project's directory, tracked, untracked or ignored, by AC-S03-3's record. Why git ignores a file (a committed pattern, `.git/info/exclude`, a user-level excludes file) no longer matters.
     - The one exception is a closed exempt list beside the checks. **Departure:** its entries are paths or patterns, not only directories, and each carries one of three reasons:
       - the gate's own recipe rebuilds it from a committed lock on every run (`.venv`);
       - it is a cache or an output a tool writes and no check reads as an input (`__pycache__`, the linters' and test runners' caches, coverage and build output);
       - it is a record the gate or the runner writes about itself (`.codegraph/gate-memory.json`, the runner's own files).
     - An entry may name exceptions that stay in the key: `node_modules` apart from its installed manifest, `.codegraph/` apart from the database and its write-ahead file.
     - The closed-list test holds two things. Every ignore line the factory generates, for every shape, is either covered or matches an exempt entry with its reason. A check script that reads a path under an exempt entry, outside its named exceptions, fails the test.
     - An ignored directory nobody listed is hashed whole. An ignored directory that is itself a repository, outside the exempt list, is AC-S03-12's case.
     - Two examples are owed first, each failing for its own reason: the adversary's `scratch_*.py` under a committed pattern, and the `.git/info/exclude` module that breaks `check-imports`.
     - The moved-key line names *a file git ignores* as its own part.
     - **SC-001 is measured again** on AC-S03-20's project after the fixes, and the number replaces the one in the quickstart. One second or more is D75 rule 7's failed demo.
     - **Residual, stated on the page and in the cruise report:** a file edited by hand inside an installed dependency tree whose manifest did not move. This is the believed half of A1. D73 rule 6 and D75 already accept it, and CI installs afresh.
  2. **A2, fixed. Closes T022.**
     - Where `git rev-parse --show-prefix` is not empty, the tracked files, the untracked files git does not ignore, the index entries and AC-S03-12's refusals are taken for the whole repository.
     - **Departure:** ignored files are covered only under the project's directory. A check walks its own project and asks git about the repository, and git never reports an ignored file.
     - The stamp stays one per project, and two projects never read each other's.
     - Example owed: the adversary's sibling edit on a `slice/<id>` branch runs the full gate.
     - D36 is untouched. It is about where a run writes.
  3. **A3 and A4, fixed (amends D73 rule 3 and AC-S03-5).**
     - Every ref git lists joins the key with what it names, tags and replace refs included.
     - The bytes of the repository's own configuration file join it, and of the worktree's where there is one, with absence a value.
     - User-level and system git configuration stay in AC-S03-31's residual, and the page names git there.
     - The older part of A3, a tag that switches `check-slice-scope` off, goes to the Parking Lot behind the PRD's slices (D39).
  4. **B1 with B2, fixed.**
     - The note carries a token only that run's two halves share. `record` writes nothing where the note's token is not its own or it was given none, and says one line: another run of the gate started here, or this was not a run of the gate.
     - The last run to start is the only one that can record, and only for the key its own checks began on.
     - **Constraint on the mechanism:** what is stored is a random value or a digest, never a process id, host or path as itself (the constitution's persisted-data MUST; T033's precedent).
     - Examples owed: the adversary's interleaving, with run B failing and with run B interrupted, and a hand-typed `record` after a failed run.
  5. **`make verify MAKE=/bin/true`, declined, with the consequence named.**
     - **Departure:** by my reading of the recipe, not run, that command also reaches `record` and writes a stamp for a tree no check judged. The stage's summary did not say this.
     - Declined on D72's A6 precedent. It takes a person deliberately replacing make, the same person can write the stamp file by hand, forcing or deleting clears it, and the trunk and CI never read it.
     - It is a line in the cruise report.
  6. **B3 to B6, fixed.**
     - B3 with B4: git's answer for a path loses exactly one trailing line feed and nothing else.
     - B5: by D72's rule, an empty directory at the note's path is removed as itself and a full one is named as the directory to delete. A file where the factory's directory should be is named as that file.
     - B6: a stamp whose instant is not exactly the UTC-to-the-second shape AC-S03-1 prints is no stamp.
  7. **C1, fixed. Graded `MEDIUM`.**
     - It is not `CRITICAL` on the log's scale: no actor's data reaches another and no actor gains a role. It breaks AC-S03-21 on an unchanged tree only, before any commit exists, never in CI.
     - (a) A `HEAD` that names no commit is not eligible: no stamp before a repository's first commit, and nothing is said.
     - (b) **Departure, closing the class.** Where `HEAD` names a commit and the name the trunk resolves to has no ref (no usable recorded `ci.branch` with one, no `main`, no `master`), the run cannot tell which branch is the trunk. It reads, writes and removes nothing, and one line before the first check says so and says to record `ci.branch` or fetch the trunk.
     - (b) amends D81 items 1, 6 and 7 for that case only. A `develop` trunk with no `main` or `master` beside it no longer stamps silently until someone reads the page. Where a stale `main` or `master` has a ref, D81 stands as written.
     - It adds no second source for the trunk. D30's resolution is used as it is.
  8. **C4, fixed. Regraded `MEDIUM`.** A team that recorded its trunk wrongly believes it is protected.
     - **The rule.** Where `project.json` is missing, unreadable or not an object, the run cannot tell which branch is the trunk. The same holds where it records a `ci.branch` and the trunk the gate resolves is not that recorded name, whatever the reason: not a string, not a branch name, a slice branch, another case, no branch here.
     - Such a run reads, writes and removes no stamp, and one line before the first check names what to fix.
     - A `ci.branch` that is simply not recorded, with `main` or `master` present, is D81's case and stamps.
     - Order of the questions: a CI marker, then a detached, unborn or non-branch `HEAD` (all silent), then cannot-tell (the line), then the trunk (silent).
     - AC-S03-21's *nothing more* gains this one line where the trunk cannot be told. D77's own condition calls that a line added, not a rule changed.
  9. **C3, fixed (amends AC-S03-23, D74 R6's last sentence and D77's fourth answer).**
     - In a stamped project `ci` depends on the checks' own target, not on `verify`.
     - By any route (a goal, a prerequisite of a user's target, any `MAKECMDGOALS`) `make ci` runs every check, reads no stamp, writes none, removes none and prints no stamp line.
     - The recipe stops reading the goals, and the forcing goal leaves the script.
     - R6's rule that `make ci` always runs every prerequisite of `verify` stands and is now held by the dependency itself.
     - Cost: the `make verify` after a green `make ci` runs in full.
     - The quickstart step that shows the forced line under `make ci` is corrected and run again by the host.
  10. **C2, fixed.** A make whose path holds a space runs the gate, records and reuses. The example uses a make reached through a directory with a space in the test tree. This is the one finding that reaches the trunk and CI. It fails closed, never green.
  11. **C5 and C6, fixed.** A `HEAD` that is a symbolic ref outside `refs/heads` is not eligible, silently. Git's reason is its first non-empty line, escaped, and one line everywhere.
  12. **C7, fixed.** The page of a project whose gate is not stamped says nothing of a stamp or `VERIFY_FORCE`. It is byte for byte what it was before the slice (D78's wording for the rule).
  13. **C8.**
     - The `-f` case is fixed: `make -f build.mk verify` in a project with no file named `Makefile` runs the gate and records, the sub-make being given the file the gate ran from.
     - The doubled `lint` is stated and goes to the Parking Lot for `S04`. It runs more, never less.
  14. **The CI systems that set no marker.** The stage's recommendation, plus one line.
     - One sentence on the page and in the fragment's catch-up: a pipeline that sets none of the three sets `CI=1` itself.
     - A line in the cruise report for a person to review.
     - **Added:** a Parking Lot line to widen D32's list across the three scripts together, behind the PRD's slices.
     - `unavailable: a run on Jenkins, Azure Pipelines, TeamCity or AWS CodeBuild showing which variables each sets and whether its checkout is attached`.
  - **Does anything need a person?** No.
    - C3 removes no check and changes nothing the merge root or CI runs. `make ci` runs the same checks and stops trusting or leaving a record.
    - A1, A2, A3, A4, C1 and C4 only make a branch run the full gate more often.
    - Item 14 is the nearest case. Constitution line 33 holds on its letter there, because every change moves the key and gets a full run. It is listed for a person, as D81 item 7 was.
  - **Level.** MINOR stands and `VERSION` stays `1.6.0.dev0`. The fragment's catch-up gains three things: `make ci` records nothing, an unusable `ci.branch` is now said, and the `CI=1` sentence.
  - **Order.** A1, B1 and C2 first, then C1 with C4, then the rest. Each is its own RED, GREEN, REFACTOR.
  - **Not an ADR.** Every part reverses by a refactor. The stamp and note change shape before any release carries them, and a stamp lacking a field is no stamp.
- **Why:** The developer has to be able to believe a green that came back in half a second.
  - A1 and B1 are each a reused green for a tree the gate fails, reached by ordinary work: a scratch file, or a second terminal. Priority 5 calls that wrong, and both are the slice's own machinery.
  - A looked-for list of ignored paths can never see a walk. A key that covers everything except what is named with a reason fails towards running the gate.
  - C1 and C4 are the trunk class. D74 would not take an arguable reading of the NON-NEGOTIABLE MUST, so a run that cannot tell which branch is the trunk does not stamp, and says the one thing to fix.
  - C3 makes the stand-in for CI what priority 5 says CI is, by a dependency no variable can talk round.
  - C2 gives back a gate that ran before the slice.
  - The rest are a line or a few lines each in files the fixes already open, which is D63's rule and D53's and D72's practice.
  - What is declined or parked can never make a trunk or CI run check less.
  - **Read, not run:** `reuse`, `record`, `eligible`, `stamp_directory` and the two lists in `assets/toolkit/scripts/verify-stamp.py`; the recipe in `src/slipwai/project/gate.py`; the `ci` rule in `src/slipwai/project/makefile.py`; the ignore lines in `src/slipwai/project/gitignore.py`.
  - **Not verified:** I ran no reproduction. What each exempt entry must be is the plan's to settle from the recipes. The cost of walking ignored files is unmeasured until item 1's re-measurement. The PRD artifact was not consulted.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** item 1's re-measurement puts the reuse run at one second or more on AC-S03-20's project because of the ignored files. A1's rule is then decided again, with D73's fallback record for those files as the first candidate, and every other item stands.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (S03's criteria on what the key covers and the exempt list, the whole repository, every ref and the repository's configuration, the run token, the paths' whitespace, the stamp's instant, the unborn and unresolvable trunk, the extended gate, the makefile the gate ran from, and the page's sentences); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/adversary-log.md` (S03's findings table with grades and states); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/slices/S03-verify-stamp/tasks.md` (one task per fixed item, the open subdirectory task closed); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (the Parking Lot: the doubled lint for the parallel gate, the tag that switches the scope check off, the wider marker list); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/slices/S03-verify-stamp/quickstart.md` (the extended-gate step and the new measurement); `changelog.d/verify-stamp.md` (the catch-up); the cruise report when it is written (the replaced make, the hand-edited dependency, the unmarked CI systems)
- **Status:** standing

## D84 — With D54 answered by the owner (D82), what do S24's criteria hold: which jobs fetch, which answers fail in CI, and what becomes of the third example?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S24-ci-fetches-slice-base · **When:** 2026-10-04T08:03:04Z · **Iteration:** 12
- **Question:** Iteration 8 left S24's gaps review open with no criterion written, waiting on D54. The owner answered it with option (a) (D82). The review's open points: which jobs fetch history and in what form; which of `check-slice-scope`'s answers in a forge's checkout become failures and which keep exit 0; what the line says on a CI that is neither GitHub nor GitLab; what the third example (*every answer is today's*) becomes; whether any other check in the `verify` job changes its answer once history is there; the level, the fragment and the catch-up note.
- **Options:** (a) write the criteria from the standing decisions as they stand — D54's own text for its option (a), D31's and D32's *Would reverse if*, D35's placement of *could not compare* and of *git could not read this checkout*, D82's sentence about the fragment — **what the stage recommended in iteration 8's note (*answered by standing decisions once D54 is, to be written as criteria then*)**; (b) reopen any of them with a skipper round.
- **Decision:** (a). **Fetch (D54, D82):** `fetch-depth: 0`, unconditional, on the `verify` job's `actions/checkout@v6` in the generated workflow and in the adopted delivery workflow; `variables: GIT_DEPTH: "0"` on `verify-delivery` in the adopted GitLab job; the integration jobs, `CONTAINER_CHECKOUT`, both smoke jobs, and the event-model, deploy and `ux-gates` workflows untouched; no `rules:` added. **The checker (D31, D32, D35):** in a forge's checkout, by either route, every no-base answer and *could not compare* become exit 1 with the *NOT checked* line, which now also says what a CI that is neither forge needs — a full clone with the trunk's branch fetched; it still prints no `git fetch`. *Git could not read this checkout* keeps exit 0 (D35: a person's, and nobody has said). A branch that is not `slice/<id>` and a developer's checkout answer as before. **The third example** is D54's rewrite: `check-slice-scope`'s answer is today's, and `check-migrations` and `check-flags` answer as on a developer's full clone; the three scripts' own rules do not change, and tests run the shipped scripts on the forge-shaped checkout at full history and at depth 1. **The sweep:** of every script under the toolkit's and the targets' `scripts/` that asks git for a merge base or a ref, only those three change their CI answer with history; `check-codegraph` runs whole under a CI marker (D46), a verify stamp is never read in CI (D74), and `check-ux-gates` reads history only under `UX_GATES_SINCE` in its own workflow. **Level and words:** PATCH, `VERSION` unchanged (D54); one fragment saying the four things D54 lists and, plainly, that `check-migrations` and `check-flags` now hold their rules in CI (D82), with the catch-up paragraph D54 wrote; the unreleased sentence in the S22 fragment that CI exits 0 is amended. **Stated, not run:** what a real forge's runner leaves in the checkout is read from its documentation and cited in the plan; this run pushes nothing (D12), so the tests build that checkout with git.
- **Why:** The developer's pull request is now held in CI to what their own `make verify` holds, which is what the owner approved twice (D39, D82) and what priority 1 asks. Every point above was already decided in words written for this moment — D31 (*answer 3 becomes exit 1 with the same line in the commit that changes the workflows*), D32 (*this arm becomes exit 1 with the others in that slice*), D54 (*this entry becomes (a) with no second skipper round*) — so a new round would only restate them. The one answer nobody has given, a gate that cannot ask git at all failing in CI, stays as it is and goes to the cruise report beside D35's line. Not an ADR: reversing it is a refactor of two generator functions, one branch of `check()`, their tests and a fragment.
- **Decided by:** host (standing decision D82)
- **Confidence:** high · **Would reverse if:** the forge's documentation, read at the plan stage, says a runner with the key set does not leave the trunk's ref in the checkout — then the form of the fetch is reopened before anything is built; or the owner says a gate that cannot read the checkout must fail in CI too.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (AC-S24-1 to AC-S24-13); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (S24's row, graph row and Parking Lot line)
- **Status:** standing

## D85 — What do converge pass 1's findings on S24 become (T013–T016)?
- **Stage:** convergence (pass 1) · **Slice:** S24-ci-fetches-slice-base · **When:** 2026-10-04T08:26:42Z · **Iteration:** 12
- **Question:** The pass converged with no `CRITICAL` or `HIGH` and appended four tasks. T013 (`MEDIUM`): the comment written above the checkout, and the fragment, name `check-flags` in every project, and that script ships only with the `aws` and `azure` targets — a generated project with another target and every adopted repository has none. T014 (`MEDIUM`, older than the slice): with a pull request's `GITHUB_HEAD_REF=slice/S1` exported beside the CI markers, one test that runs a whole adopted gate fails, at the commit before the slice as after it. T015 (`LOW`): the fragment says a developer's machine answers as before, and a local shell with a CI marker exported and no base now fails; `check()`'s docstring still calls the flag *a developer's failure*. T016 (`LOW`): nothing kept holds what `migrate` does with the step, or the `ux-gates` workflow's checkout. Which are fixed before the demo, how is the comment worded, and where does T014 go?
- **Options:** (a) fix T013, T015 and T016 now, before the demo, and place T014 in the Parking Lot — **what the stage recommends** (`delivery/commands/drive.md`: a `MEDIUM` or `LOW` rides along or lands in Phase 4; this run's lesson from iteration 10: fix leads before the demo where they change what the demo follows; D39: a defect older than the slice goes behind the PRD's slices unless `CRITICAL`); (b) fix all four in S24; (c) leave all four to Phase 4.
- **Decision:** (a). **T013:** one wording in every project, no branch on the target: the comment names `check-slice-scope` and `check-migrations`, which every generated and adopted gate runs, and says *and `check-flags` where the project has one*; the fragment says which projects have it (a production target of `aws` or `azure`), and a test holds, over every backend and target and an adopted repository on each forge, that each check the comment names without that clause is in the `verify` chain of the Makefile beside it. **T015:** the fragment's sentence gains the clause — a local shell with `CI`, `GITHUB_ACTIONS` or `GITLAB_CI` exported and no trunk to compare with now fails as CI does — and the docstring says the flag is a failure on either checkout. **T016:** a kept test for what `migrate` does with the step (the key arrives where the step is as generated; a step the project rewrote conflicts) and one assertion on the `ux-gates` workflow's checkout. **T014:** not S24's — it fails the same way before the slice, it is one capability more (constitution V), and this repository has no slice pull request under D12; a Parking Lot line, for the completion audit and for whoever opens the first one.
- **Why:** The developer reads the comment in their own workflow file and the fragment after `migrate`; a comment naming a check their project does not have is a small untruth in the one place they look to learn why the job fetches everything (owner brief, *Taste*: plain words, true where printed). Naming the two checks every gate runs and qualifying the third keeps one sentence for every project with no setting and no branch in the generator (constitution III). T015's clause is the same honesty for the one developer D32 already described. T016 keeps a criterion from resting on a run nobody can repeat. T014 changes nothing a user sees and nothing CI checks.
- **Decided by:** host (stage recommendation)
- **Confidence:** high · **Would reverse if:** a person wants the comment exact per project — then `workflow()` is told whether the target ships `check-flags` and names it only there.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/slices/S24-ci-fetches-slice-base/tasks.md` (T013–T016); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (Parking Lot)
- **Status:** standing

## D86 — What does each of the seven after-converge gaps findings on S24 become before the demo (G1–G7)?
- **Stage:** gaps (after converge) · **Slice:** S24-ci-fetches-slice-base · **When:** 2026-10-04T08:40:04Z · **Iteration:** 12
- **Question:** The gaps pass (`drive-gaps`, read-only, over `838a3b0..53f4611`) reported seven findings. G1 (graded `HIGH` by the delegate): `check-migrations.py`'s `merge_base()` tries only `main`, `origin/main`, `master`, `origin/master` and never reads `ci.branch`, so in a repository whose trunk is `develop` and which still has a `master`, the slice's full fetch makes CI compare with `master` and refuse an expand and its contract landed separately on `develop` — on a push to the trunk and on every pull request. ## D87 — Where does each of the adversary's eight findings on S24 go (F1–F4, B1–B4), and how is each one that is fixed closed?
- **Stage:** Phase 4 (adversary triage) · **Slice:** S24-ci-fetches-slice-base · **When:** 2026-10-04T09:04:10Z · **Iteration:** 12
- **Question:** Two seams at `edb78cd` confirmed eight findings, none `CRITICAL`. Seam A, on `check-slice-scope` in a forge's full-history checkout: F1 (HIGH) — `older_of()` returns the base under the name the branch recorded in `ci.branch` when that base and the pull-request target's base share no history, so an orphan root merged into the slice and pushed as `evil` passes a `Makefile` edit, *compared with `evil`*, exit 0; F2 (MEDIUM) — D30's *the older base wins* lets a pull request that puts host files back to an old trunk commit pass, where that commit's `project.json` records another trunk name and the author pushes that name there; F3 (MEDIUM, older) — the fetch the gate prints resolves `main` to a tag before the branch; F4 (LOW, the red is new) — a checkout whose remote is not `origin` gets *NOT checked … needs `fetch-depth: 0`* with nothing saying which ref was looked for. Seam B, on the other two gates with history: B1 (MEDIUM) — a pull request to `release/1` whose expand landed there earlier is refused, because both gates compare with the trunk; B2 (MEDIUM) — an expand squash-merged to `main`, then the contract on the same branch without merging `main` back, is refused on a source-tip checkout; B3 (LOW) — a `git revert` restoring an expand and its contract removed in one commit is refused; B4 (LOW, older) — `delivery_workflow()` writes `branches: [{branch}]` unquoted. Where does each go, how is each fixed one closed, and does closing F1 need a person?
- **Options:** (a) fix in S24, through failing tests, what makes a sentence of S24's untrue or is new with it; state in the fragment what is a reading; place outside what is older and another capability — the host's recommendation (F1 and F4 now, F3 now if it is one line, F2 parked as a question for a person, B1 and B2 as catch-up sentences and into S31's row, B3 and B4 parked); (b) fix all eight in S24; (c) park all but words; (d) `unavailable: a person's approval` for F1.
- **Decision:** (a), the host's recommendation taken whole, with F3 fixed now. **F1 — fixed now, no person needed. The rule:** on a pull request whose target is usable and has a base, where the base under the trunk's name and the base under the target are different commits, neither an ancestor of the other, and `git merge-base` finds no commit common to both, the target's base is the one compared with, and the line names the target and says the pull request targets it, as it does wherever the target's base won. In code this is the last fallback of `older_of()` returning its second argument instead of its first; nothing else in `merge_base()` moves. The invariant the fix closes the class with: on a pull request with a usable target that has a base, the commit compared with is always the target's base or an ancestor of it. Not *no base*: an unrelated *target* is no base (D35, T024), but here the target is sound and only the branch-chosen name is unrelated, so failing the run would hand the author a way to make every such pull request say *NOT checked* instead of the refusal it has earned. **Between a recorded name and `main` with no target:** no rule is added — `merge_base()` never compares those two, a push pipeline has only D30's local promise (its stated residual), and that stands. **Criterion (the next free; the host numbers it, AC-S24-14 by the brief):** *Given the checkout of AC-S24-4, where the slice's commits record `ci.branch: evil` and edit `Makefile`, merge an orphan root commit carrying the slice's own tree, and that root is pushed as branch `evil`, with `GITHUB_BASE_REF=main` (and the same on GitLab's two variables), when `check-slice-scope` runs, then it exits 1 naming `Makefile` and `project.json`, compared with `main` at the commit where the branch left `main`, in the words it uses where the pull request's target won; the same refs on a slice that stays inside its scope pass with that line; and in every pull-request checkout with a usable target that has a base, the commit compared with is the target's base or an ancestor of it — where the two bases share no history, the target's.* **F4 — fixed now, as words (AC-S24-15):** *Given a forge's checkout with no usable base, then the NOT-checked line names the two refs it looked for, `refs/heads/<trunk>` and `refs/remotes/origin/<trunk>`, and its last clause reads "on any other CI, a full clone with the trunk's branch fetched from a remote named `origin`"; given a full-history checkout whose only remote is `upstream`, the line is that one, exit 1; no base selection changes and no other remote is read (D30: only the two full names answer).* **F3 — fixed now (AC-S24-16):** *Given any line in which the gate prints a fetch, then the command is `git fetch origin refs/heads/<name>:refs/remotes/origin/<name>`; given a remote with a branch `main` and a tag `main` at the slice's head, when the printed command is run and the gate re-run, then the slice is compared with the branch, not the tag.* AC-S24-9 gains one clause: *except the printed fetch, which names the branch in full (AC-S24-16)*. **F2 — not fixed; Parking Lot and the cruise report as a question for a person.** It is D30's rule working as written, and D30's own *Would reverse if* says making the target the base outright is a person's. **B1, B2 — words in the catch-up, and a clause in S31's row for its gaps stage.** No script changes by a byte (AC-S24-8). AC-S24-8's reading gains both cases; the hold tests in `tests/test_ci_history_gates.py` gain one each (a pull request to `release/1` whose expand landed there earlier: refused; the squash-then-continue branch: refused at the source tip, passes on the merge commit), so S31 starts from tests it must change. **B3, B4 — a Parking Lot line each.** **Fragment, third paragraph of `changelog.d/ci-fetches-slice-base.md`,** after *…a full clone with the trunk's branch fetched*: *from a remote named `origin`. The line names the two refs it looked for, `refs/heads/<trunk>` and `refs/remotes/origin/<trunk>`: a job that clones under another remote name has the history and is still told NOT checked until the trunk is there under `origin`.* In the same paragraph, *on a developer's machine every answer is what it was, except that* gains a first exception: *the fetch the check prints now names the branch in full — `git fetch origin refs/heads/<trunk>:refs/remotes/origin/<trunk>` — so a tag of the trunk's name is never fetched in its place, and*. The unreleased `changelog.d/slice-scope-base.md` (line 27) quotes the old command and is amended to the new one. F1 adds no fragment sentence: it makes *a slice pull request is held to its scope in CI* true. **Fragment, inside the single Catch-up paragraph,** after *…or seed the new flag `off`.* and before *Where the trunk is not `main` or `master`…*: *Where CI checks out the branch's own tip and not its merge with the trunk, a branch whose expand already landed by squash or rebase and which carries on with the contract is still refused, because the branch's own copy of the expand is what the check sees: merge the trunk into the branch, or rebase onto it, and the contract passes. A pull request that targets a branch other than the trunk — a release branch — is compared with the trunk all the same, in CI as on a full clone: everything that branch has landed since it left the trunk counts as new, so a contract is refused there although its expand reached that branch in an earlier pull request, and so is a flag declared there earlier and seeded other than `off` now. It clears once the trunk carries the expand, or the flag's declaration, and that branch has merged the trunk.* The paragraph's existing *Nothing in `project.json` changes this yet* then covers both. **S31's row, appended to *Defers*:** *S24's adversary pass (D87, B1, B2) leaves two cases for this gaps stage: a pull request to a non-trunk branch is charged with everything that branch landed since it left the trunk, and a source-tip checkout of a branch behind a squash-merged expand is refused until it merges the trunk — the pull request's target as a candidate answers the first.* **Parking Lot lines:** (1) *What S24's adversary pass left (D87). F2: on a pull request, `check-slice-scope` compares with the older of the recorded trunk's base and the target's (D30); where an old trunk commit's `project.json` records another trunk name and a branch of that name sits there, a pull request that puts everything outside its scope back to that commit's tree is compared with it and passes — the trunk's host-surface changes since are undone unseen (run: `/tmp/s24-adv-a/t4`, exit 0, compared with `master`). Needs the recorded name to have changed in history and a wholesale revert. Whether the target is the base outright on a pull request is a person's (D30's Would reverse if); for the cruise report.* (2) *B3: a pull request that restores, with `git revert`, an expand and its contract that both landed and were removed in one commit is refused by `check-migrations` as new in the same change; restore them in two pull requests. Older rule, newly red in CI. For the completion audit.* (3) *B4: `delivery_workflow()` in `src/slipwai/project/adopted_ci.py` writes `branches: [{branch}]` unquoted, so a trunk named `a,b`, `1.0`, `true` or `{x}` parses to something else and `x]`, `!x`, `%x` is a YAML error, with `adopt` exiting 0. Older than S24, another capability (D39); quote the name, with a test over those names.* **The question for the person, ready to send:** *On a pull request, `check-slice-scope` compares a slice with the older of two commits: where it left the trunk `project.json` records, and where it left the pull request's target (D30). A pull request can pass by restoring host files to an old trunk commit if the recorded trunk name changed in history and a branch of the old name is pushed there. Should the pull request's target be the base outright? That closes it, and changes what CI checks; a slice whose recorded trunk and target are different long-lived branches would then be held against the target alone.* **Tasks, in order, each its own RED-GREEN:** F1; F3; F4; the two hold tests; the fragments, the docstrings of `older_of()`, `fetch_command()` and `not_checked()`, and the module docstring last. **Adversary log:** F1, F3, F4 *fixed* with their commits; F2 *question — Parking Lot, a person's*; B1, B2 *stated in the fragment; S31*; B3, B4 *declined here — Parking Lot*. PATCH stands, `VERSION` unchanged.
- **Why:** A developer trusts a green CI run on a slice pull request to mean the slice stayed in its lane, and S24 is the slice that makes CI look at all; with F1, any author can switch that off with one pushed branch, on the exact checkout AC-S24-4 describes. **F1 needs no person** for three reasons. D30 already promises it in words (*in CI on a pull request against the trunk, nothing the branch commits or pushes moves the base forward*), so the fix makes the code keep an approved promise rather than changing what CI is meant to check. It only adds a refusal in a state that promise forbids. And D35 closed the same class (A1, B1) on the host's word, by the same reasoning. S24's *Defers: any change to what the checker holds* is not crossed: what the checker holds is unchanged; the fallback that let it hold nothing is. D30's reserved question — the target as base outright — is left exactly where D30 left it, which is why F2 goes to a person and F1 does not: in F1 *oldest* has no answer and the code guessed in the author's favour; in F2 *oldest* has an answer and it is the rule. The target is chosen over the recorded name because it is the one thing the branch cannot change (D30's own reason for counting it). **F3** is one string in `fetch_command()` and the assertions that quote it; D30 says a tag named `main` never answers, and the gate's own printed advice installed one — a command the gate hands a person is the gate's word (owner brief, *Taste*; D34's G1 and D35's T027 fixed the same line twice for the same reason). S24's new sentence sends a CI of one's own to fetch the trunk's branch, and this is the only fetch the product prints. Both fragments are unreleased, so the release says it once. **F4** is the Taste rule that a gate says what it checked in one line a person can act on; reading other remotes would be base selection, which is D30's. **B1, B2** are the developer's-machine answer arriving in CI, which is what the owner approved (D82), and D86 took the mirror case the same way: words where a maintainer reads after `migrate`, the fix in S31 behind its own approval. B2's sentence is written for *a checkout of the branch's own tip* and names no forge, because what a GitLab merge-request pipeline checks out was assumed by the adversary and is not verified here. **B3, B4** are older and another capability (constitution V; D39). Nothing here removes or loosens a check (constitution I; owner priority 1). **Run by the skipper,** in the adversary's fixtures and one scratch directory since removed: F1 in `/tmp/s24-adv-a/t1/checkout` with the shipped script byte-identical — exit 0, *compared with `evil` at 504ca74*, while the diff against `main`'s base shows `Makefile` and `project.json`, and `git merge-base` of the two refs exits 1; F2 in `t4/checkout` — exit 0, *compared with `master` at 51637b2*; F3 against `t3/origin.git` — the short form fetched the tag's commit, the `refs/heads/` form the branch's; B1 (`hist/c3g`), B2 (`c2l` refused, `c2g` passes) and B3 (`c4g`) with `check-migrations` — exits as reported. **Read, not run:** F4 (from `bases_of()` and `not_checked()`); B4 (line 91 of `adopted_ci.py`); `check-flags` in B1; that merging the trunk clears B1 and B2 (from `merge_base()` and the `--diff-filter=A` call in `check-migrations.py`). The PRD artifact was not consulted. Not an ADR: reversing any of this is a refactor of three functions, their tests and two fragments — no schema, contract, dependency or stored shape.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** a person shows an honest repository where a slice's base under the recorded trunk and its base under the pull request's target share no history and the recorded one is rightly compared with — F1's rule then becomes *no base, NOT checked* there and the choice is theirs; or F3's string turns out to reach beyond `fetch_command()`, its assertions and the two fragments, in which case F3 alone goes to the Parking Lot and AC-S24-9 keeps its wording.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (AC-S24-14 to AC-S24-16; AC-S24-8 and AC-S24-9 amended); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/slices/S24-ci-fetches-slice-base/tasks.md` (the tasks above); `changelog.d/ci-fetches-slice-base.md`; `changelog.d/slice-scope-base.md`; `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (S31's row; Parking Lot); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/adversary-log.md`; `assets/toolkit/scripts/check-slice-scope.py` and `tests/test_ci_history_gates.py` (at the tasks); the cruise report (F2's question)
- **Status:** standing

## D88 — How is a gate target that writes kept from running beside a check that reads or writes the same thing under `make -j`, on which make versions, and which targets are the writers?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S04-parallel-gate · **When:** 2026-10-04T11:12:00Z · **Iteration:** 13
- **Question:** FR-002 and the Edge Cases say writers are *`.NOTPARALLEL` locally*. The gaps stage read GNU Make's NEWS and ran it: `.NOTPARALLEL` with a target named keeps that target from running beside others on no version (before 4.4 it serialises the whole makefile; from 4.4 it serialises the named target's own prerequisites). So how is a writer kept apart, on which versions, and which targets are writers? Three borders ride on the answer: (i) `check-python` is *Fail, first* today and is not first under `-j`; (ii) what an adopted repository's gate does under `make -j`, and whether that is in S04; (iii) what becomes of the literal words in `spec.md`.
- **Options:** (a) the literal directive, `.NOTPARALLEL: <writers>` — serial everywhere before 4.4, no isolation from 4.4; (b) ordering by prerequisite, which every make since 3.81 honours, with FR-002 re-worded to what is held and the adopted gate declared serial as a whole by a bare `.NOTPARALLEL:` — **the host's recommendation**; (c) `.WAIT` between phases, 4.4 only; (d) `verify`'s recipe runs the phases as successive sub-makes.
- **Decision:** (b), the host's recommendation, taken, with three additions the evidence and the generators call for (rules 3, 5 and 8). No part is `unavailable`. As rules a test can hold:
  - **R1, the rule.** A target of the gate that writes, inside the working tree, something another target of the gate reads or writes never runs beside that target. It is kept apart by being its prerequisite (or a prerequisite of a prerequisite), and by nothing newer than GNU Make 3.81 documents: no `.WAIT`, no `.NOTPARALLEL` with a target named, no `--output-sync`.
  - **R2, the writers the criteria hold, in a generated project.**
    - *The toolchain sync of each backend family* (Python's `uv sync` into each service's `.venv`; the other families' `install` command, including whatever in-tree file its first run writes, such as Go's `go.work.sum`). It is one target, run once per make invocation. `lint`, `typecheck`, `test`, `check-openapi` and every other target that runs a tool out of that environment name it as a prerequisite, and inside the gate none of them syncs again. `check-openapi` is on the list because it runs `uv run --no-sync` and today finds the environment only because `lint` happens to precede it. This is also how FR-003's *once per invocation* is met for `make`; `./scripts/verify` called directly still syncs once itself.
    - *The root `npm ci` and `build-packages`* (`node_modules/`, `packages/*/dist`). They are already prerequisites and stay as they are.
    - *The model tooling's install* (`scripts/event-model/node_modules/` and its lock). It becomes one target that `check-drawio`, `model`, `model-drawio` and `model-drawio-test` each name, so two of them asked for in one `make -j` install once. Whether the install is skipped, and against which lockfile, is FR-003's and not this entry's.
  - **R3, a backend whose three native checks share one build directory.** Both Java backends run Maven for `lint`, `typecheck` and `test`, and each run writes the service's `target/` (read in `src/slipwai/project/native_commands.py`, not run). There those three are ordered among themselves by prerequisite and run beside only the checks that do not touch `target/`. The Python, TypeScript and Go three run together after their sync. How the order is spelled is the plan's; if a standalone `make test` on a Java project thereby runs more than it does today, the fragment says so.
  - **R4, not writers for this rule.** These need no ordering:
    - a tool's own cache that only that tool reads (`.ruff_cache`, `.mypy_cache`, `.pytest_cache`, Vite's and Vitest's caches);
    - `__pycache__`, which the interpreter writes per file by rename with identical bytes whoever writes it;
    - anything outside the tree (`check-openapi`'s `mktemp` file, the uv, npm, Go and Maven caches, which their tools lock themselves);
    - `check-codegraph`'s memory file (one writer, no reader among the checks, atomic replace);
    - Go's `coverage.out` (written and read inside the one `test` recipe);
    - the stamp, which `verify`'s recipe writes after the sub-make has returned.
  - **R5, the extended gate is not promised.** `make -j ci` is outside S04. Its `migrate` before `test-integration` is list order today and fails loudly, never green, when broken. One Parking Lot line records it, and the gate's page says `-j` is for `verify`. `migrate` and `dev` take the sync target as a prerequisite in place of their own sync line only if the plan finds that free.
  - **R6, (i) `check-python`.** It is a prerequisite of the sync target and of every gate check that runs `python3`, so on every make version it finishes before any of them starts. On a `python3` older than 3.10, `make -j verify` prints its one line, starts no other check and exits non-zero. A standalone `make check-imports` there now gets the same line, which is an improvement and not a removal.
  - **R7, (ii) the adopted gate, in S04.** Where `stamped()` in `src/slipwai/project/gate.py` answers no (a wrapped application, or the delivery material moved, which is D74's R7 and D78's line), the generated Makefile carries a bare `.NOTPARALLEL:` with no prerequisites. Every make since 3.81 reads that as *this makefile runs serially whatever `-j` says*. `make -j verify` there runs the recorded commands and the ratchet one at a time, in today's order with today's output, and `baseline.json` has one writer at a time. Nothing else in that Makefile changes. A recorded command that itself calls `make` runs that application's own makefile under whatever jobs make hands it; that is the application's, and the adoption page says so in one sentence. The fragment carries the experimental label and the catch-up: `slipwai migrate` brings the line and asks nothing else.
  - **R8, the `.specify/` edge case.** No target of the gate writes under `.specify/` (`check-agents`, `check-extensions` and `check-speckit` only compare), and a test holds that. `agents` and `install`, which do write there, are not in the gate, and asking for them beside it in one `make -j` is not promised.
  - **R9, (iii) the words in `spec.md`.** Both sentences are re-worded to what is held:
    - **FR-002** becomes: *The gate's checks MUST be declared so `make -j verify` runs concurrently every check that writes nothing another reads, with the same results as the serial run; a target that writes what another target of the gate reads or writes MUST never run beside it, on every GNU Make from 3.81, by being ordered ahead of it as a prerequisite (D88). The gate of a repository that adopted the method MUST run serially whatever `-j` says.*
    - **The edge case** becomes: *`make -j` and a target that writes `.specify/`: no target of the gate writes there, and a test holds it; `agents` and `install`, which do, are not in the gate, and running them beside it in one `make -j` is not promised (D88).*
    - The S04 row in `story-split.md` loses *writers `.NOTPARALLEL` locally* for *writers ordered ahead as prerequisites; the adopted gate serial*.
    - *Same results* means the same checks run, each gives the same verdict, and the exit code and the closing `verify: all gates passed` line match. It does not mean the order of lines, nor on a red tree which failing check is named first. AC-S03-29's *in the order and with the output it had* is re-worded to that for a `-j` run and kept as written for a serial one.
  - **Does a person need to be asked?** No. CI and the merge root run `make verify` with no `-j`. Under that command every check that ran still runs and none is removed. `check-python` is still first. The serial order of the others moves only in that the sync now precedes `lint` instead of opening it. The adopted gate's line does nothing without `-j`.
- **Why:**
  - *The directive fails its own purpose.* The developer asks `make -j verify` for two things: the gate in half the time (6.2 s to 3.2 s on the stage's project) and a verdict that can still be believed. Option (a) gives a developer on macOS's 3.81 or Ubuntu's 4.3 no speed at all, and on 4.4 it lets three `uv sync` runs write one `.venv` at once — the false-green-or-flaky gate the owner brief calls the one thing that makes the work pointless (priority 5).
  - *The other options.* (c) breaks the Makefile's first line and AC-S03-29's 3.81 clause. (d) hides the order in a recipe where `make lint` alone cannot see it, and the sync would be skipped or repeated depending on the door used.
  - *A prerequisite is the plain answer.* It is the oldest thing make does; the project already uses it for `npm ci` and `build-packages`, with a comment saying why; and it makes FR-003's *once* true by construction.
  - *Why R3 and `check-openapi` are added.* The rule has to be true of every starter the factory generates (constitution I), not only the one the stage ran. A Java gate with three Maven runs in one `target/` is the same defect as three syncs in one `.venv`.
  - *Why the adopted gate is serial, and in S04.* Its commands are ones a person recorded for code the factory did not write (D74's reason for R7). Nobody here can call them read-only, and today `make -j verify` there already runs three ratchets against one `baseline.json`. One line that every make honours gives that developer today's results and takes nothing away. The path is experimental, and speed there can be added later.
  - *Why the literal words go.* A requirement that names a directive which does not do the thing would have the judge passing a gate that holds nothing.
  - *Verified and not.* Re-run here on GNU Make 4.4.1: the toy `M1` (three targets start together despite `.NOTPARALLEL: b`, 1.0 s) and `M3` (the prerequisite alone first, 2.0 s). Read, not run: the Java, Go and TypeScript recipes; GNU Make 3.81 and 4.3; that 3.81 hands `-j` to the quoted `"$(MAKE)"` sub-make in `verify`'s recipe; the PRD artifact was not consulted.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** a make the Makefile promises (3.81 on macOS, read and not run) is shown not to hold a prerequisite ahead of its dependents inside the gate's sub-make, or not to hand `-j` to that sub-make. The order then moves into `verify`'s recipe as successive sub-makes (option d), and R1's wording stands.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (FR-002 and the `.specify/` edge case re-worded; AC-S03-29's order clause qualified; the criteria below, numbered by the host); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (the S04 row's FR-002 words; a Parking Lot line for make -j ci and for the adopted gate's speed); the slice's plan, tasks and changelog fragment when they are written; at the tasks, `src/slipwai/project/gate.py`, `src/slipwai/project/native_commands.py`, `src/slipwai/project/adopted_targets.py` and the Makefile generator beside them
- **Status:** standing

## D89 — Who gets the parallel gate, what does a `make -j verify` run print and promise, what does the serial run keep, what is measured, and does the stamp stand across the two?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S04-parallel-gate · **When:** 2026-10-04T11:12:00Z · **Iteration:** 13
- **Question:** FR-002 and User Story 1's fifth scenario promise that `make -j verify` runs every check the serial run ran, in any order, with the same results and a matching exit code. Unstated: (1) whether plain `make verify` stays serial, and whether the ladder's text or the generated CI workflow change what they type; whether a job count is the owner brief's *anything that changes what the merge root or CI checks*; (2) what a person can read off a parallel run and what *the exit code matches* compares; (3) what the serial run keeps of AC-S03-29's *in the order and with the output it had* once a sync may move ahead of the checks; (4) whether a speed-up criterion is written, of what, on which project, held by whom; (5) whether a pass under `-j` is reused by a serial run and the other way round.
- **Options:**
  - (1) (a) the gate is made correct under `-j`, a person or harness asks for it, the page says so, the ladder's text and CI do not change — **the stage's recommendation**; (b) `verify` passes a job count to its sub-make by default; (c) (a) plus the ladder's text typing `-j`.
  - (2) (a) same set of checks, exit status zero exactly when the serial run's is, the last lines name every failed check through make's own error lines, no stamp on failure, each check's output grouped where the make can — **recommended**; (b) byte-identical output sets; (c) say nothing.
  - (3) (a) the serial run keeps every check, the closing line and the checks' order among themselves; only the one sync phase may newly come first — **recommended**.
  - (4) (a) one demo-measured criterion in AC-S03-20's shape, parallel not slower than serial, no fixed ratio — **recommended**; (b) a fixed ratio; (c) none.
  - (5) (a) stands, the job count is not in the key — **recommended**.
- **Decision:** The stage's recommendation on all five, with one departure in part 2 (rule 6) and four additions (rules 3, 4, 9 and 11). Each rule is one a test or the demo can hold.
  1. **Who gets it: (1a).** Plain `make verify` runs its checks one after another, as today. The checks run at once only when the person or harness asks, by `make -j verify` or through `MAKEFLAGS`. The gate never asks for jobs itself and there is no new setting.
  2. **Nothing else types it in this slice.** The ladder's commands, the cruise text and the workflow `src/slipwai/project/ci_workflows.py` writes keep typing `make verify`. Whether the ladder types `-j` goes to `S06-scoped-gate`, which owns that text; the host writes a Parking Lot line.
  3. **Is a job count a person's question? No.** A job count changes how many checks run at once, never which checks run or what they answer; D73 rule 7 read `UX_GATES_JOBS` the same way. Under rule 1 CI and the merge root type what they typed and run every check they ran. CI asking for jobs itself is not taken in this slice and comes back as its own question if ever proposed.
  4. **What the claim covers.** `verify` as the only goal. `make -j verify lint`, `make -j ci` and every other target under `-j` are not claimed, and the page says so. An exported `MAKEFLAGS` reaches those targets as it did before this slice.
  5. **Same results: (2a), read as follows.**
     - The same set of check commands starts under `-j` as in the serial run of the same Makefile on the same tree.
     - The exit status is zero exactly when the serial run's is zero. A non-zero status is make's own and is not compared number for number.
     - A passing run ends with `verify: all gates passed` as its last line, under `-j` too.
     - A run in which any check failed prints no closing line and leaves no stamp (D76 rule 5, unchanged).
     - Byte-identical output (2b) is refused: the logs already differ in timings (`0.54s` and `0.57s`, `[12ms]` and `[14ms]`).
  6. **Failed checks — the departure.** The recommendation says the run's last lines name every failed check. They do not. Run here on GNU Make 4.4.1, with and without grouping: the `*** [a] Error 1` line was followed by `Waiting for unfinished jobs....` and then the other check's whole output. So:
     - Every check that failed is named by make's own `***` line; with grouping it follows that check's output directly.
     - A run that did not pass, serial or parallel, ends with one line of the gate's own beginning `verify:`. It says the gate did not pass and that each failed check is named above on a line beginning `make: ***`.
     - Under `-j` more than one check may fail, and the check the serial run would stop on may not have started. The claim is that every failure that happened is named, not that the two runs name the same one.
  7. **Grouping.** Where the make running the gate lists `output-sync` among its features, the sub-make groups each check's output by target, so no check's line is cut by another's. Where it does not (3.81), lines may interleave under `-j`. The option is never passed to a make that lacks it. A serial run's lines still appear as they are produced (run here: two lines one second apart arrived one second apart with the option set).
  8. **The page.** The page the project gets about the gate says in plain sentences:
     - `make -j verify` runs the checks at once, and when to use it: when you wait on the gate locally.
     - Each check's output appears when that check finishes, so a long test run shows no progress until then.
     - On a make older than 4.0, lines may interleave.
     - The claim is for `verify` alone (rule 4).
  9. **Writers and FR-002's `.NOTPARALLEL`.** The stage's evidence shows `.NOTPARALLEL` with a target does not keep that target from running beside its siblings on any version. FR-002's clause is therefore read by its purpose: under `-j`, no two processes of the gate write the same path at once. It is held by a mechanism GNU Make 3.81 has; the mechanism is the plan's. The host amends FR-002's wording to that outcome.
  10. **What the serial run keeps: (3a).** Every check, the checks' order among themselves, each check's own output, and the closing line. What may change: each toolchain's sync or install runs once ahead of the checks that need it, and `check-drawio` prints a line saying it skipped its install where it did (FR-003).
  11. **`check-python` stays first, both ways.** Its description is *Fail, first*. Serial or under `-j`, with a `python3` older than 3.10 the run fails on `check-python` before any sync or other check starts.
  12. **A target run alone still syncs.** `make lint`, `make typecheck` and `make test`, each typed alone, sync their toolchain once before running.
  13. **The measurement: (4a).** One criterion, no new success criterion in the owner's list and no fixed ratio.
      - The project is AC-S03-20's: Python backend, no frontend, target `none`, real toolchain, warm (just passed), on a branch other than the trunk with no CI marker.
      - `VERIFY_FORCE=1 make verify` and `VERIFY_FORCE=1 make -j verify` are each run three times at the demo.
      - Both sets of numbers go into the slice's quickstart and the fragment, with the command, the machine and its core count.
      - The criterion is that the median under `-j` is lower than the serial median. If it is not, the demo failed; the criterion is not revised.
      - The suite holds the cause without a clock, with stand-in executables written in the test tree: under `-j` two checks are running at the same moment, and the sync is launched once per toolchain.
  14. **The stamp: (5a), stands as D73 to D76 wrote it.** The job count is not in the key. A pass under `-j` is reused by a serial run and the other way round. D73 rule 8's second key check still refuses to record a pass during which a covered file moved.
  - **Unavailable:** nothing is decided on a missing fact.
  - **Not verified:** GNU Make 3.81 was not run (none on this machine), so rules 7 and 9 on 3.81 rest on the stage's reading of make's NEWS. Only the Python project was run under `-j`. The speed-up on AC-S03-20's project is unmeasured until the demo; the stage's 6.2 s against 3.2 s is from a project with a browser app.
- **Why:**
  - **Part 1.** The developer wants the wait back but must still believe the gate. Owner priority 4 puts `-j` before the scoped gate and the merge tree as its own small slice, and priority 1 keeps the merge root and CI as they are. A default job count (b) would run three syncs at once on one environment on every fresh clone, CI's included, before anyone has seen it hold there. CI's log read one check after another is worth more than three seconds.
  - **Part 2.** A person reads a failed gate from the bottom, and so does a delegate that tails the log. The owner's taste is a gate that says why it failed in one line a person can act on. Under `-j` the bottom of the log is whichever check finished last, so the gate has to say where to look. Printing that line in serial runs too keeps the two runs' own lines the same and costs one echo.
  - **Part 3.** The serial order is what a person and S03's pinned suite know. The sync moving ahead is the whole of FR-003, and naming it as the only change keeps SC-007 (identical findings) checkable.
  - **Part 4.** A clock in the suite would be flaky or too loose, as D69 and D75 found. The gain depends on cores and on the longest check, so a ratio would be a number nobody chose.
  - **Part 5.** Rules 5 and 9 are what make a pass under `-j` the same verdict as a serial pass, so one stamp for both removes more repeated runs (priority 2) and vouches for nothing more.
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** medium · **Would reverse if:** a check is found whose answer on the same tree differs between a serial run and a run under `-j` for a reason the slice cannot remove (two tools sharing one cache or environment that cannot be ordered by a prerequisite). That check is then run alone ahead of the others, as the sync is. If it cannot be, part 5 reverses: a pass under `-j` is not recorded.
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (FR-002's writers clause, User Story 1's fifth scenario and Independent Test, AC-S03-29 amended, S04's criteria); `specs/001-faster-slipwai/story-split.md` (S04's row, and a Parking Lot line for S06 on whether the ladder types the job flag); the slice's plan, quickstart and fragment when they are written (the mechanism for rules 7 and 9, the demo's two measurements); the suite beside `tests/test_verify_stamp_pinned.py` (the pinned order, amended for the sync phase)
- **Status:** standing

## D90 — What is the invocation that syncs once (FR-003), and what does each way into a Python mode do afterwards?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S04-parallel-gate · **When:** 2026-10-04T11:12:00Z · **Iteration:** 13
- **Question:** FR-003 says `./scripts/verify` MUST sync each toolchain once per invocation, and the slice's example says one run of the script runs `uv sync` once. Read to the letter that already holds. The cost is that one `make verify` calls the script three times (`lint`, `typecheck`, `test`), each syncing every Python service again, and under `-j` all three at once on one `.venv`. Five parts: (1) what *the invocation* is and what the count is per; (2) what a mode typed alone does, and whether a mode may ever run on an environment nobody synced; (3) `make migrate` and `make dev`, which write out two syncs; (4) what FR-003 asks of TypeScript, Go and Java in S04, and whether they are run or read; (5) what a person sees.
- **Options:** (a) the invocation is one `make` run: the sync is one target every Python-running target names as a prerequisite, the recipes call the modes without a second sync, and a mode reached any other way syncs first as today — the host's recommendation; (b) memoise inside the script with a marker file newer than the lock; (c) leave three syncs and only serialise them under `-j`.
- **Decision:** (a), the host's recommendation, with two things made exact: how the skip is handed over, and what `make verify lint` counts.
  1. **The invocation.** It is one `make` process building the goals it was given. In it, each Python service's environment is synced exactly once, before any Python mode starts, however many of `lint`, `typecheck`, `test`, `format`, `adversarial`, `test-integration`, `migrate`, `dev` the run reaches. That covers `make verify`, `make -j verify`, `make ci`, `make lint test`. The sync is one phony target those targets name as a prerequisite, using only what GNU Make 3.81 documents: no `.WAIT`, no `.NOTPARALLEL` with prerequisites. The count is per Python service: N services means N starts of `uv sync`, each `--project` once. `make install` reaches its Python sync through the same target, so `make install test` syncs once. **Stated reading:** `make verify lint` is two make processes (the gate's sub-make and the outer goal), so it syncs at most twice, as D83 left `lint` running twice there — more, never less. Run directly with no mode, the script syncs once, as today.
  2. **A mode on its own.** `make lint` syncs once, through the prerequisite, then lints. `./scripts/verify --lint-only`, or any mode typed directly, run by a CI step, or run by an agent's hook, syncs first exactly as today. The skip is a second **argument** on the script that only the Makefile's own recipes pass. It is never an environment variable, and it is never the default. Every spelling published outside the Makefile stays the syncing one: the commands in `native_commands.py`, the CI workflow's steps, hooks and docs. No supported way in runs a mode on an environment nobody synced. If the sync target fails (lock and manifest disagree, `uv` missing), no Python mode starts, and the failure is said once instead of three times. The name of the argument is the plan's.
  3. **`make migrate` and `make dev`** (Python) take the same prerequisite and lose their written-out `--install-only` line; each syncs once. Go's and Java's install step inside those recipes is not touched.
  4. **Other backends.** The reading: FR-003's *sync* is a step that builds a service's environment from its committed lock before a mode runs.
     - TypeScript **holds already**: `npm ci` is the recipe of the file target `node_modules/.package-lock.json`. S04 pins that with a test that reads the generated Makefile, and changes nothing.
     - Go and Java are **not applicable**: their gate modes carry no such step (`go mod download` and the Maven readiness guard sit in `install`, `migrate` and `dev` only). The criterion's note says so, and S04's FR-003 part changes no line of theirs.
     - Run or read, for FR-003 only: Python is **run**, both with a stand-in `uv` for the count and with the real `uv` for a full `make -j verify`. TypeScript, Go and Java are **read**. What FR-002's `-j` needs run for them is that question's, not this one's.
     - An adopted repository's recorded commands are its own and are not rewritten.
  5. **What a person sees.** Nothing new from the script: the sync stays `--quiet`. Make echoes the sync target's recipe line once, as it echoes every other recipe. A failed sync prints uv's own message once.
  - **The observable a test counts.** A stand-in `uv` first on `PATH`, a script in the test tree that appends its arguments to a log and exits 0 (a fake, no mocking framework). The count is the log lines whose first argument is `sync`, grouped by `--project`. The order is that every `sync` line precedes the first `run` line.
- **Why:** The developer pays this three times per gate and about three gates per slice. Priority 2 says fewer runs of the same thing on the same content before anything cleverer, and a prerequisite is exactly that, in make's own terms. It is already how `npm ci` is done in the same file, so the project has one idea of *install once* instead of two. The prerequisite also takes three writers off one `.venv` under `-j`: FR-002 needs that from this slice anyway, and on a fresh clone nobody has shown it safe. Option (b) is a cache that can be stale: a marker newer than the lock says nothing about a `.venv` someone edited or half-built, and priority 5 calls a cache that could cache a false green wrong. Option (c) delivers none of FR-003's saving. The skip is an argument rather than a variable because a variable is ambient. It could sit in a shell profile or a pipeline's environment, be inherited by everything a recipe starts, and silently turn every mode into one that never syncs. It would then have to join the stamp's key in `verify-stamp.py` or risk a false green. An argument exists only where someone wrote it. The stamp's reason for exempting `.venv/` (*the gate's own recipe rebuilds it from a committed lock on every run*) stays true, with once per run in place of once per phase. Nothing the merge root or CI checks changes: the same checks run, and `--locked` still refuses a lock that disagrees with its manifest. So this is not a question for a person. Go and Java get a stated *not applicable* rather than an invented step (Principle III).
- **Decided by:** drive-skipper (claude-fable-5-1)
- **Confidence:** high on parts 1, 2, 3 and 5; medium on part 4's run-or-read split. `mvn` is not on this machine's `PATH`, and I read `go.py` and `java.py` only through `native_commands.py` and the evidence file, not line by line. · **Would reverse if:** a supported way into a mode is found that bypasses both make's prerequisite and the script's own sync — for example, a generated hook or CI step that must call the Makefile's non-syncing spelling. The skip then goes, and the modes sync every time behind a serialising prerequisite (option (c) for that path).
- **Written to:** `specs/001-faster-slipwai/spec.md` (S04's criteria and its *Gaps reviewed* note, with the two readings: make verify lint, and *not applicable* for Go and Java); the slice's plan under `specs/001-faster-slipwai/slices/S04-parallel-gate/`; at the tasks, `src/slipwai/project/languages/python.py`, `src/slipwai/project/makefile.py`, `src/slipwai/project/native_commands.py`, and the comment on the `.venv/` exemption in `assets/toolkit/scripts/verify-stamp.py`; a PATCH fragment under `changelog.d/` with the migrate catch-up sentence
- **Status:** standing

## D91 — Which lockfile does `check-drawio` match, what is *matches*, what installs, what does a skipped install say, which targets take the rule, and where does the split of `verify-stamp.py` go?
- **Stage:** slice gaps · **Slice:** S04-parallel-gate · **When:** 2026-10-04T11:12:00Z · **Iteration:** 13
- **Question:** FR-003 says `check-drawio` MUST skip `npm install` when the installed tree matches a committed lockfile, and the slice's example says it *skips the install and says so*. A generated project commits no lockfile for the model tooling: `scripts/event-model/` ships a `package.json` only, four targets run `npm --prefix scripts/event-model install …` on every run, and the first run writes an untracked `scripts/event-model/package-lock.json`, which also leaves a first stamped pass unrecorded. Six parts: (1) which lockfile, at what level, and what `slipwai migrate` does for a project made before; (2) what *matches* is; (3) `npm ci` or `npm install`; (4) what line is said and when; (5) whether `model`, `model-drawio` and `model-drawio-test` take the same rule; (6) whether the split of `assets/toolkit/scripts/verify-stamp.py` (910 lines; S03's T023, placed on S04 by D81) rides in S04.
- **Options:** 1–3: (a) the factory ships a committed lock, one file target in the root's pattern, `npm ci` — the host's recommendation; (b) no shipped lock, skip when `node_modules` is newer than `package.json`; (c) compare content each run with a small script. 4: (a) one `check-drawio:` line when the install is skipped, the other three silent — recommended; (b) silence everywhere and the example rewritten. 5: (a) all four on the file target — recommended. 6: (a) a method slice of its own in the pool behind the PRD's slices — recommended; (b) ride in S04 as the Parking Lot says; (c) leave the line as it is.
- **Decision:** 1–3 (a), 4 (a), 5 (a), 6 (a) — the host's recommendations taken, with one correction of fact in part 1 and one sharpening in part 6.
  1. **The lockfile.** The factory ships `scripts/event-model/package-lock.json` beside the manifest, committed, in every project with the event profile (under the layout's own prefix where the delivery material is moved).
     - **Level: MINOR, said out loud.** `AGENTS.md`'s table names *a new generated file* under MINOR. S04's row in the split is amended from PATCH to MINOR. `VERSION` stays `1.6.0.dev0`, already a MINOR ahead of 1.5.1, so nothing is raised. The slice's fragment claims MINOR on its first line.
     - **How the lock is made.** By npm against the public registry, from the three pins in `package.json`, in a directory with no `node_modules` (`npm install --package-lock-only`). It is never written by hand.
     - **What the plan must verify, not assume.** Every `resolved` begins `https://registry.npmjs.org/`. `lockfileVersion` is 3. The root entry's dependencies equal the three pins, held by a factory test that needs no network. Every platform package of esbuild is listed (the lock this machine's gate wrote in `/tmp/s04-gaps/pyshop` has 26, all from registry.npmjs.org), so the gate installs on macOS and Windows from a lock made on Linux. `npm ci` with `--prefix` accepts the lock and leaves `git status` empty.
     - **If the registry cannot be reached when the lock is made:** `unavailable: a lock generated by npm against registry.npmjs.org`. The slice parks; it does not ship a guessed integrity hash.
     - **What `migrate` does — the correction.** `migrate` does not replace an untracked file. It refuses on any uncommitted change (`git status --porcelain`, in `src/slipwai/migrate.py`), and an untracked lock is one, today as before. The fragment's catch-up therefore says three cases.
       - *No lock in the project:* the merge brings it; nothing to do.
       - *An untracked lock from an earlier gate run:* delete it before `slipwai migrate` (it was never the project's record; `migrate` refuses while it is there, as it does for any untracked file).
       - *A lock the project committed itself:* the merge stops on that file as git leaves it. Take the factory's side (`git checkout --theirs scripts/event-model/package-lock.json`) and commit, unless the project changed the tooling's `package.json`, in which case regenerate the lock from its own manifest.
     - **A fourth catch-up sentence.** A project that edited the tooling's `package.json` must now commit a lock that agrees with it, because the install refuses otherwise.
     - No generated file is deleted or renamed, so nothing here is the brief's *Always ask a person*.
  2. **Matches.** The root's rule, as it stands: the file target `scripts/event-model/node_modules/.package-lock.json` depends on `scripts/event-model/package.json` and `scripts/event-model/package-lock.json`, and is touched after a successful install. The tree matches when that marker is newer than both. No content comparison and no new script.
  3. **The install.** `npm ci` with `--prefix scripts/event-model` and today's three quiet flags, spelled once, in that rule. It writes nothing tracked and nothing untracked beside the manifest, so the first stamped pass is recorded — the Parking Lot's *lock-matching install settles* is this. `npm install` appears nowhere in the model's targets.
  4. **Says so.** When `check-drawio` runs and the rule did not install in this invocation, it prints one line before the check: `check-drawio: the model tooling matches scripts/event-model/package-lock.json; not reinstalled`. When the rule did install, the line is absent and make's own echo of the `npm ci` command is what is seen. The other three targets say nothing about a skip, as the root's targets do. The mechanism is the plan's, with one condition: it is decided inside the one make invocation and holds on GNU Make 3.81. A variable set by the install recipe and read when `check-drawio`'s recipe is expanded did this on 4.4.1 in a scratch makefile, under `-j4`. If no mechanism holds on 3.81, the line is said only where it can be said truthfully and the example is re-worded; a line that could be wrong is not shipped.
  5. **All four.** `check-drawio`, `model`, `model-drawio` and `model-drawio-test` take the file target as a prerequisite and lose their install line. The note in `src/slipwai/project/model_targets.py` that the install is *repeated per recipe rather than shared through a prerequisite* is rewritten to say the opposite and why. The Mermaid CLI's on-demand prefix under `.mermaid-cli/` is not touched (D68, D70 stand).
  6. **The split of `verify-stamp.py` does not ride in S04.** This overrides D81's placement of T023 and the Parking Lot clause that S04 *splits it along the four parts T023 names*. Nothing else of D81 moves.
     - It becomes a method slice of its own, a row the host numbers, in the pool behind the PRD's slices (D39).
     - **The sharpening:** it is a prerequisite of whichever slice next names that script in its plan, and lands as its own pull request before that slice. That includes S04, if S04's plan turns out to need the script after all. If no slice names it, it is taken in pool order, at the latest before the completion audit.
     - T023's three conditions carry into its row: one path the `Makefile` calls, one thing `migrate` delivers, and the closed-list test still importing the lists from where they are defined. Its level is for its own gaps stage.
  - **Not an ADR.** Going back to `npm install`, or changing the skip rule, is a refactor of one generator module, its tests and a fragment. No dependency is added (the same three pins), and no schema, identity or published contract moves.
- **Why:** A developer driving slices pays `check-drawio`'s 0.7 s install on every gate, about three gates a slice, for a tree that has not moved. They also get a stray untracked file after their first gate, which makes the first stamped pass unrecorded and makes `migrate` refuse.
  - **Why ship the lock.** The owner's requirement says *a committed lockfile*, and there is none; option (b) leaves nothing to match and the stray file keeps appearing. Constitution XI wants a dependency lockfile in version control and no floating versions. Today `tsx`'s transitive packages float on every fresh install, so two machines can render with different tooling.
  - **Why the timestamp rule.** It is the rule the root already trusts, and S03's stamp already describes it: its cache list keeps `node_modules/.package-lock.json` in the key as *what npm installs, which the recipe installs only when the lock is newer*. A content comparison is not a byte comparison — npm's hidden lock here has 5 package entries against the committed lock's 31 — so (c) is a normalising script, a new mechanism where an existing rule will do (Taste; constitution III).
  - **Priority 5.** The marker is a make dependency, not a verdict. A checkout, a `migrate` or an edit that moves either manifest dates it newer than the marker and forces `npm ci`. `npm ci` removes the tree before installing, so an interrupted install leaves no marker.
